— A-234 — 
In calculating the financial obligations of the KCMSD under 
the Court’s overall plan for which the district lacks funding, the 
Court finds that the KCMSD suffered a deficit of $1,092,365 in 
1986-87 in implementing the long-range magnet school plan 
based on its obligation of $4,158,136 and expenditures of 
$3,065,771. In addition, the Court approved a total of $60,234,436 
in capital improvements for the long-range magnet plan in its 
orders of November 12, 1986 and April 29, 1987, with KCMSD’s 
share set at $30,117,218. 
KCMSD has currently expended 
$3,027,413 for these improvements in 1986-87 but is without 
revenues to fund the balance of its share of these capital improve- 
ments, or $27,089,805. 
For fiscal year 1987-88, KCMSD projects an operating bud- 
get of $121,342,253 and total revenues of $1 10,558,418, creat- 
ing a deficit of $10,783,835. Included in the operating budget is 
approximately $7.15 Million for additional teachers and for 
salary increases for present teachers, $2,240,000 for facilities 
maintenance, and $869,189 for special education students. The 
AFT 691 has moved the Court to order a teacher’s salary 
schedule larger than that included in the KCMSD 1987-88 
operating budget. 
The Court finds that the KCMSD has an obligation not only 
to eliminate the effects of unlawful segregation but also to insure 
that there is no diminution in the quality of its regular academic 
program. Bradley v. Milliken, 540 F.2d 229, 245 (6th Cir. 1976), 
aff'd, 433 U.S. 267 (1977), citing Hart v. Community School of 
Brooklyn, 383 F.Supp. 699, 741 (E.D. N.Y. 1974), aff'd, 512 
F.2d 37 (2d Cir. 1975). Therefore, it is essential that the KCMSD 
have sufficient revenues to fund an operating budget which can 
provide quality education, including a high quality faculty. The 
Court is quite aware that the KCMSD’s teacher salary position, 
in comparison with the surrounding school districts, has deterio- 
rated significantly because of KCMSD’s failure to obtain pas- 
sage of a tax levy to fund teacher salary raises. Consequently, 
— A-235 — 
KCMSD’s ability to attract new teachers and retain present 
teachers has been restricted. While the revised salary schedule 
proposed by the KCMSD would not equal those in suburban 
districts, it will certainly make the KCMSD more attractive to 
new teachers and more pleasing to its present teachers. As stated 
at the beginning of the order, the Court will not order either of the 
salary proposals submitted by the KCMSD and AFT 691 be- 
cause this decision properly lies within the discretion of the 
school board. However, the Court will provide KCMSD with 
revenues of $7.147 Million per fiscal year through 1991-92 to 
fund teacher salary increases, and revenues of $3,109,189 per 
year to fund the costs of facilities maintenance and special 
education students. 
The KCMSD is also without resources to finance its portion 
of the 1987-88 desegregation budget, $15,427,329, which in- 
cludes the 1987-88 implementation cost of the long-range mag- 
net school plan. In addition, the KCMSD is without the funds to 
pay its proposed 
share of the 1987-88 transportation 
costs of the 
magnet plan, estimated by it to be $573,518. KCMSD is also 
obligated in 1987-88 to fund $6,069,801 in capital improve- 
ments pursuant to the long-range capital improvement plan 
approved by the Court. Finally, the Court estimates that site 
selection and student relocation costs associated with the long- 
range capital improvement plan will be approximately $4 Mil- 
lion per year, with KCMSD’s portion to be $2 Million per year 
(affidavit of Roger Gaunt). In summation, the KCMSD has 
financial obligations under the Court’s overall desegregation 
plan for 1986-87 and 1987-88 of $62,509,653 for which it lacks 
resources to fund. 
In fiscal year 1988-89, the KCMSD is obligated to fund an 
estimated $4,454,203 for implementation of the long-range 
magnet school plan and $27,399,873 in capital improvements 
pursuant to the long-range capital improvement plan. The Court 
projects that KCMSD’s portion of the magnet school transpor- 

— A-236 — 
tation costs for each of the next four fiscal years will be $573,518. 
The Court also projects that the KCMSD will have an operating 
budget deficit of $10,256,835 for fiscal years 1988-89 through 
1991-92 due to expenditures needed for teacher salary increases, 
facilities maintenance, and special education students. In addi- 
tion, the Court estimates that the 1988-89, 1989-90, 1990-91, 
and 1991-92 desegregation 
budgets at approximately $44,000,000 
per fiscal year, excluding the long-range magnet costs approved 
by the Court in its November 12, 1986 order, because of the 
continuing cost of the remedial programs previously ordered by 
the Court. Therefore, the Court finds that the KCMSD will 
experience a deficit of approximately $11,000,000, or 25% of 
the desegregation budget, in each of those fiscal years. 
In 
summation, the Court projects a total deficit of $53,684,429 for 
the KCMSD for 1988-89. 
Similarly in 1989-90, the Court projects that the KCMSD will 
experience a deficit of $5,027,721 in long-range magnet costs, 
$63,555,647 in capital improvement costs, $10,256,835 in oper- 
ating budget, and $1 1,000,000 in desegregation budget, totaling 
$89,840,203. Using the same method of calculation, the pro- 
jected deficits for years 1990-91, and 1991-92 are $42,543,837 
and $33,823,793 respectively, bringing the total deficit to 
$282,401,915. A summary of the KCMSD’s known and pro- 
jected obligations through 1991-92 under 
the Court’s desegrega- 
tion plan is set forth in Attachment B. 
In its motion for funding relief, the KCMSD requested the 
Court to enjoin the rollback in the real estate tax levy otherwise 
required under Proposition C (R.S. Mo. § 163.050 and 164.013). 
The KCMSD also requested the Court to order the State of 
Missouri to advance to it funds for its desegregation and opera- 
tion expenses that it is unable to fund. The KCMSD 
would then 
repay the State when resources were available. 
The Court will not require the State of Missouri to fund any of 
KCMSD’s financial obligations under the court-ordered deseg- 
— A-237 — 
regation programs. However, the United States Court of Ap- 
peals for the Eighth Circuit stated clearly that the desegregation 
remedy ordered by this Court shall be fully funded. Jenkins 
v. 
State of Missouri, 807 F.2d 657, 686 (8th Cir. 1986), and has 
discussed in detail the procedure the district court is to follow in 
achieving this goal. Liddell v. State of Missouri, 731 F.2d 1294, 
1319-23 (8th Cir. 1984). 
The record clearly shows that the KCMSD is unable with its 
present resources to raise revenues to fund its share of the costs 
assessed under the desegregation orders. 
The KCMSD has 
exhausted all available means of raising additional revenue, 
including presenting a bond issue in 1987 and tax levy increase 
proposals to the voters in four separate elections in 1986 and 
1987. The KCMSD has not had a bond passage 
or a levy increase 
since 1969. As a result, its physical facilities have literally 
rotted. 
Because of KCMSD’s inability to raise additional funds under 
the present system, the Court encouraged the Missouri General 
Assembly to “explore the possibility of enacting legislation that 
would permit a district involved in a desegregation plan more 
versatility than it presently has to raise funds with which to 
support the program.” November 12, 1986 Order at p. 7. Such 
legislation was introduced but was received unfavorably and 
ultimately failed. In addition, the State of Missouri and the 
KCMSD have been unable to agree on an alternate method of 
raising KCMSD’s share of the desegregation costs. Therefore, 
the Court has explored all the alternatives set forth by the Eighth 
Circuit and is left with no choice but to exercise its broad 
equitable powers and enter a judgment that will enable the 
KCMSD to raise its share of the cost of the plan and therefore 
insure that the constitutional violations committed by the KCMSD 
and the State of Missouri 
are cured. 
The United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit has 
stated that such relief may only be granted after the Court has 
—

— A-238 — 
conducted an evidentiary hearing. Liddell v. State of Missouri, 731 
F.2d 1294, 1323 (8th Cir. 1984). However, the plaintiffs, the 
KCMSD, 
and the State of Missouri have stated that they do not re- 
quest an evidentiary hearing on this issue, and consent to the Court’s 
entry of a judgment based on the record developed in this case. 
A district court’s broad equitable power to remedy the evils of 
segregation includes the power to order tax increases and bond 
issuances. Liddell v. State of Missouri, 731 F.2d at 1322. The 
United States Supreme Court has stated that a tax may be 
increased if “necessary to raise funds adequate to . 
.. operate and 
maintain without racial discrimination a public school system.” 
Griffin v. School Board of Prince Edward County, 377 U.S. 218, 
233, 84 S.Ct. 1226, 12 L.Ed.2d 256 (1964). 
A studied estimate of the additional revenues needed by the 
KCMSD to meet its share of the desegregation costs during the next 
five years reveals the sum of approximately $150,000,000 for cap- 
ital improvements and $135,000,000 for other desegregation costs. 
In its order of June 14, 1985, this Court stated that it was 
reluctant to take any action to increase taxes. The Court now 
affirms the statement made in its order of August 25, 1986 that 
it is not insensitive to the fact that those patrons of the KCMSD 
who voted in previous elections have refused to approve a tax 
levy increase and a bond issue. However, a majority has no right 
to deny others the constitutional guarantees to which they are 
entitled. This Court, having found that vestiges of unconstitu- 
tional discrimination still exist in the KCMSD, is not so callous 
as to accept the proposition that it is helpless to enforce a remedy 
to correct the past violations. Failure of the KCMSD to come 
forward with its share of funds to implement the remedial plan 
would certainly operate to hinder vindication of federal consti- 
tutional guarantees to which the school children in the KCMSD 
are entitled. This Court cannot shrink its sworn duty to uphold 
the Constitution of the United States (p. 4). The Court must 
weigh the constitutional rights of the taxpayers against the 
— A-239 — 
constitutional rights of plaintiff students in this case. The Court 
is of the opinion that the balance is clearly in favor of the students 
who are helpless without the aid of this Court. 
The Court is of the firm conclusion that it has no alternative but 
to impose tax measures which will enable KCMSD to meet its 
share of the cost of the desegregation plan. At the Court’s request 
the parties have submitted estimates on revenues which would 
be generated through various tax measures. 
The plaintiffs have moved the Court to require KCMSD to 
issue $144,175,000 in general obligation bonds, to add a $1.73 
property tax levy in the KCMSD, and to impose a 1/2% sales tax 
effective January 1, 1989 within the KCMSD 
to permit KCMSD 
to fund its share of the costs of the desegregation orders. 
During the hearing on the liability issue in this case there was 
an abundance of evidence that many residents of the KCMSD 
left the district and moved to the suburbs because of the district’ s 
efforts to integrate its schools but continue to be employed in the 
district. After careful consideration, the Court has determined 
that it would be equitable to involve these people in a plan to help 
defray the district’s desegregation expense. 
The Court will impose a 1.5% increase as a surcharge 
on the 
Missouri State Income Tax (raising the present rate on individu- 
als from 6% to 7.5%) on residents and nonresidents of the 
KCMSD, including business associations, partnerships and cor- 
porations 
who earn salaries, wages, commissions 
and all other 
compensation and income subject to the Missouri State Income 
Tax for work done, services rendered and business or other 
activities conducted within the KCMSD. This surcharge shall 
become effective for all income earned after September 25, 
1987. All employers and persons presently legally responsible 
for withholding the Missouri State Income Tax shall have the 
same responsibility for withholding the 1.5% increase. The 
State through its Department of Revenue shall collect the in- 

— A-240 — 
creased tax and remit it to the KCMSD within thirty days after 
its receipt of same. KCMSD shall promptly prepare and publish 
a legal notice of this income tax increase (surcharge) giving 
notice to all employers within the district and to all persons and 
entities who receive an income for activities performed within 
the district. 
Revenue generated by the income tax surcharge shall be used 
to retire capital improvement bonds which are herein authorized. 
It is anticipated that the surcharge will create sufficient revenue 
for the KCMSD to meet its portion of the cost of the Capital 
improvement plan. The surcharge shall remain in effect until 
such time as the bonds are retired or until other provisions are 
adopted to insure their retirement. 
The KCMSD presently has a tax levy of $2.05 per $100.00 
assessed valuation. This is much less than the tax levy of any 
neighooring school district. In order to fund the other desegre- 
gation costs other than capital improvements for which it is 
obligated the district needs approximately $27,000,000 addi- 
tional per year through the 1991-92 school year. A property tax 
increase of $1.95 per $100 assessed valuation would generate 
about $27,000,000 annually. Therefore, the Court will order the 
property tax levy to be increased to $4.00 per $100 assessed 
valuation through the 1991-92 fiscal year. 
The KCMSD is directed to issue capital improvement bonds 
in the total amount of $150,000,000 to be retired within 20 years 
from the date of issue. The board is authorized to issue credit 
enhanced leasehold revenue bonds and general obligation bonds 
in such proportion as the school board, in its discretion, deter- 
mines to be most advantageous to the district. 
In accordance with the foregoing memorandum, it is 
ORDERED that the motion of the KCMSD for the approval 
of its capital improvement plan is approved as modified herein; 
and it is further 
— A-241 — 
ORDERED that the motion of the KCMSD for approval of its 
student transportation plan for the long-range magnet school 
plan is deferred and if a hearing is necessary on the plan it is set 
to commence on Monday, December 14, 1987 at 9:00 a.m.; and 
it is further 
ORDERED that the motion of AFT 691 for alternate funding 
relief regarding the salaries of the KCMSD teachers is denied; 
and it is further 
ORDERED that the motion of KCMSD for funding relief is 
granted in part in that it is ordered that in order to fund the 
obligation of the KCMSD for the desegregation program, a 
surtax of 1.5% is added to the Missouri State Income Tax for all 
persons and entities receiving income for work done, services 
rendered, and income received from activities within the KCMSD 
and the tax levy for the KCMSD is raised to $4.00 per $100 
assessed valuation; and it is further 
ORDERED that said increased revenue shall be applied to 
satisfy the obligations of the KCMSD as set forth in this memo- 
randum. 
/s/ Russell G. Clark 
RUSSELL G. CLARK, 
DISTRICT JUDGE 
UNITED STATES 
DISTRICT COURT

APPENDIX I 
IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE WESTERN DISTRICT OF MISSOURI 
WESTERN DIVISION 
No. 77-0420-CV-W-4 
KALIMA JENKINS, et al., 
Plaintiffs, 
vs. 
STATE OF MISSOURL, et al., 
Defendants. 
ORDER 
[Filed July 25, 1988] 
On May 31, June 1, and June 2, 1988, the Court conducted a 
hearing on KCMSD’s motion for approval of the desegregation 
budget for years 4 through 6 of the desegregation plan. Plaintiffs 
also moved for the approval 
of three additional programs for year 
4. In addition, the parties filed lengthy post-trial briefs in support 
and in opposition to the submitted budgets. Based upon the 
evidence presented at the hearing and the post-trial briefs, the 
Court will approve a modified budget for years 4 through 6 of the 
desegregation plan as set forth in this order. 
District Communications 
The Court has approved a public information budget of 
$30,000 for each of the first years of the desegregation plan. The 
KCMSD requests funding of $30,350 for 1988-89, and $30,725 
for 1989-90, the slight increases needed for additional supplies. 
The Court finds that these costs are reasonable and approves the 
submitted budgets for years 1988-89 and 1989-90. The KCMSD 
requests funding 
of $45,077 for year 1990-91, $13,952 of which 
is for salaries for three student interns who would work 
in the 

—A-M6— 
communications office in conjunction with the Westport Com- 
munications Magnet that is scheduled to open in the fall of 1990. 
This program is similar to the traditional COE programs avail- 
able to KCMSD high school students except that the proposed 
interns would be paid by the KCMSD out of desegregation 
funds, rather than by a local business. The Court finds that the 
internship program is simply not a necessary cost of the district 
communications office and thus will not be approved. 
As a 
result, the Court approves a budget for 1990-91 of $31,125, 
which includes a slight increase for additional supplies. 
Research and Evaluation 
The KCMSD requests funding of $89,980 in 1988-89 for the 
purchase of anew computer System to evaluate the court-ordered 
desegregation programs. Dr. Paul Holmes, KCMSD director of 
research, evaluation and testing, testified that the data generated 
for the evaluation of the magnet schools and the other desegre- 
gation programs exceeds the memory capacity of the current 
System that he described as “two generations old.” The evalua- 
tion of the magnet schools and the other desegregation programs 
is essential to the success of the plan and the Court finds that a 
new computer system is necessary and that the submitted costs 
are reasonable. The State maintains that should the Court 
approve the purchase of the new system, that only the incremen- 
tal cost attributable to evaluating the desegregation programs 
should be funded by the desegregation budget. The Court finds 
that it is the court-ordered desegregation programs, particularly 
the large number of magnet schools that are coming on line, that 
have created the need for the new equipment and therefore that 
— 34? — 
Staff Development/Human Relations 
The KCMSD has requested funding for $482,955 in 1988-89, 
$466,885 in 1989-90 and $280,309 in 1990-91 for an extensive 
staff development/human relations program. The Court had 
approved $500,000 for staff development in year 1 of the 
desegregation plan. The present proposal actually consists of 
two components, human relations for desegregation, and im- 
provement of student achievement. The human relations staff 
development program is intended to assist all staff members, 
including teachers, administrators, custodians, etc. in promoting 
and understanding cultural diversity, reducing bias, racism, and 
discrimination, improving interracial interaction, enhancing dis- 
cipline and classroom management techniques in multi-cultural 
settings, and enhancing academic achievement of all students in 
a desegregation setting. The Court is well aware that there are 
inherent problems when teachers, students, custodians, etc. of 
varying races are placed together in an educational setting for the 
first time. However, the Court finds that the approximate $1.25 
Million proposed for the human relations component is not a 
necessary cost of the desegregation plan. The Court e 
teachers, administrators and other KCMSD personnel who face 
these problems to seek the advice and counsel of fellow staff 
members who have experience in dealing with those situations. 
firmly believes that the money proposed for this program is 
better spent in 
improving the student's knowledge of the subject 
areas tested on such examinations, and not on techniques of 
taking the examinations. For these reasons, the Court denies 
KCMSD’s motion for approval of the staff development/human 
relations program as part of the desegregation plan for years 
1988-89, 1989-90, 
and 1990-91. 

—_e 
Administrative Reorganization 
On March 8, 1988, the Board of Directors of the KCMSD 
approved Superintendent George F. Garcia’s proposed reorgani- 
zation of the administrative structure of the KCMSD. Dr. Garcia 
testified that the prior structure was really composed of two 
administrations, one for the magnet schools and one for the 
traditional schools. He testified that the new organization avoids 
duplication and will more effectively administer the Court’s 
desegregation programs as well as the traditional school pro- 
grams. The KCMSD requests the Court to approve a total of 
$472,487, which is 70% of the cost of the reorganization, as part 
of the desegregation budget for years 4 through 6 of the deseg- 
regation plan. The Court defers to Dr. Garcia’s decision to 
reorganize the administration of the KCMSD and believes that 
the new organization will more effectively administrate the 
education provided in the KCMSD. However, it is clear that the 
reorganization was done not only to provide more effective 
implementation of the desegregation programs, but also the 
traditional school programs. The KCMSD has simply not shown 
the Court that the submitted costs relate solely to the administra- 
tion of the desegregation programs and thus the Court will not 
approve the additional funds over the extensive funding already 
approved in the long-range magnet school plan for administra- 
tion purposes. 
Interest Payments 
In its order of June 14, 1985, the Court required KCMSD to 
contribute $10,000,000 and the State $27,000,00 for initial 
capital improvements to KCMSD facilities. 
The KCMSD 
requests the Court to approve the interest costs it has accrued and 
will accrue in obtaining the $10,000,000 for years 1988-89, 
1989-90, and 1990-91. In its order of July 6, 1987, the Court 
found that these interest costs were properly desegregation 
expenditures and recognized the estimated interest costs for 
1987-88 of $353,061 as a desegregation expense. However, in 
— A-249 — 
its determination of the contributions to be made by the KCMSD 
a result, the Court approves as aKCMSD desegregation expense 
$1,488,668 for year 1988-89, $838,380 for year 1989-90, and 
$788,067.50 for year 1990-91. 
The State is not required to 
contribute any of these amounts. 
Desegregation Monitoring Committee 
The Desegregation Monitoring Committee is an arm of this 
Court and has shown itself to be an 
effective and impartial group 
Specialist who will be responsible for tracking budgets as related 
to capital improvements and other desegregation program com- 
ponents, and a 5.5% increase in salary and benefits for the 
executive secretary and administrative assistant. 
The Court 
approves the DMC’s request for a transfer of $15,000 from the 
balance of the research budget for 1987-88 to the staff compen- 
sation budget for partial funding of the financial specialist in 

— A-250 — 
In addition, the DMC has informed the Court that there is a 
balance in the per diem account for year 1987-88 and requests 
permission to compensate committee members who have ex- 
ceeded their compensable number of hours from the remaining 
funds in this account. The Court authorizes such payments and 
orders that any remaining balance after these additional pay- 
ments are made should be carried over to the 1988-89 per diem 
budget. 
IMPROVING STUDENT ACHIEVEMENT 
Effective Schools 
The KCMSD has proposed a budget of $6,680,000 for the 
effective schools program for 1988-89, an increase of $125,000 
over 1987-88. The KCMSD also requests funding of $7,300,000 
for 1989-90, and $7,500,00 for year 1990-91. The State opposes 
the requested funding on the ground that the “effective schools 
program has not been demonstrated to be achieving its intended 
purpose.” State’s Post-trial Memorandum at p. 64. The Court 
finds that the State’s position is without merit. 
This Court, in Jenkins v. Missouri, 639 F.Supp. 19, 34 (W.D. 
Mo. 1985), stated that the effective schools funding must be 
spent “for the sole purpose of improving student achievement as 
measured by the Iowa Test of Basic Skills and the Basic Essential 
Skills Test (BEST).” Dr. Paul Holmes, KCMSD Director of 
Research, Evaluation and Testing, testified that the pass rate for 
KCMSD students on the BEST test has increased from 39% in 
1984-85, the year before implementation of the effective school 
program, to 59.6% in 1987-88, the highest ever in the history of 
the KCMSD. Specifically, 93.6% of KCMSD students passed 
the reading portion of the BEST test and 73.3% passed the math 
portion in 1988, as opposed to 85% and 56.4% in 1985. Dr. 
Holmes also testified that the performance of KCMSD students 
on the ITBS test has shown similar improvement. 
In 1985, 
before implementation of the effective schools program, the 
— A-251 — 
national norm on the ITBS was met or exceeded in only 29% of 
the grade level subtest comparisons. This increased to 39% in 
the spring of 1986 and 52% in the spring of 1987. The State does 
not dispute these figures but only points to an independent 
evaluation of the effective schools program in which only 29% 
of the faculty survey responded that the effective schools pro- 
gram made “a great or very great change” in their instruction. 
The Court finds that this evaluation only covered the plannin 
during year 1985-86 and the implementation of the program in 
1986-87, and despite the results of the faculty survey, the 
schools. For these reasons, the Court approves the continuation 
of the effective schools component for 1987-88 through 1990- 
91. 
past are reasonable and will approve them for years 1988-89, 
1989-90, and 1990-91. As a result, the Court approves funding 
for the effective schools program in the amount of $6,680,000 
- 1988-89, $7,300,000 for 1989-90, and $7,500,000 for 1990-

Reduction in Class Size 
The KCMSD has requested $8,787,191 for the 1988-89 cost 
of teachers required to meet the class size levels ordered by this 
Court, a $337,056 increase over 1987-88. The KCMSD has also 
em funding of $9,226,551 for 1989-90 and $9,687,877 for 
1. 

— A-252 — 
In Jenkins v. Missouri, 639 F.Supp. 19, 30 (W.D. Mo. 1985), 
the Court ordered that the maximum class size was to be attained 
by school year 1987-88 and that the total cost of the program was 
to be paid by the State and not to exceed $12,000,000. The State 
argues that through year three of the desegregation plan, it has 
been ordered to expend $12.3 Million Dollars on reduction of 
class size and therefore that it is not liable for any additional 
expenditures on this component. For the following reasons, the 
State’s position is without merit. 
First, the maximum class sizes were not attained in 1987-88. 
Secondly, at the time of the 1985 order, the KCMSD was not 
operating middle schools nor did its high schools have a seven 
period day, both of which will occur in the 1988-89 school year 
and both of which have an effect on the number of teachers 
needed to meet the requirements of the Court. 
Finally, the 
maximum class sizes must be maintained once achieved and the 
Court’s June 14, 1985 order does not preclude the Court from 
ordering the State to fund a share of the maintenance. 
The State also argues that the class size reduction should be 
based upon average rather than maximum student teacher ratios 
and should only be implemented at nonintegrated schools. For 
the reasons set forth on p. 5 of 
its June 6, 1987 order, the Court 
rejects both of these claims. 
The State also argues that even using the maximum student/ 
teacher ratios, the KCMSD has grossly exaggerated the number 
of teachers required to meet such ratios. The Court finds that the 
dispute simply is based upon differing projections of number of 
students who will enroll in the KCMSD over the next three years. 
The State assumes that no new students will enroll while the 
KCMSD assumes enrollment in the magnet schools up to the 
capacity of those buildings. The Court finds that neither 
is likely 
an accurate projection. However, the Court will approve the 
proposed budget for 1988-89 of $8,787,191, but will not approve 
the proposed increases for 1989-90 and 1990-91. The State is 
— A-253 — 
protected for 1988-89 because the KCMSD is ordered to hire 
only the number of teachers necessary to meet the court man- 
dated class size maximums and thus the State will pay only for 
its share of the teachers needed. If student enrollments justify 
additional funding for years 1989-90 and 1990-91, KCMSD 
may move the Cv .:t for such funding. 
The KCMSD is directed to submit a report to the Desegrega- 
tion Monitoring Committee by October 15, 1988 setting forth the 
actual enrollment in the KCMSD broken down by K-3, 4-5, and 
6-12, and the number of teachers hired by the KCMSD under this 
component. The report shall also include the specific number of 
students in each classroom of K-5 students for the school year 
1988-89. 
Summer School 1988 
The KCMSD has requested $1,793,397 for the summer school 
program in 1988. This figure consists of $1,302,571 to operate 
the elementary, middle, and senior high summer programs, and 
$506,730 for transportation for summer school programs at nine 
magnet schools, including the 1986-87 magnets. The magnet 
transportation component will be considered under the 1986-87 
magnet section. 
In Jenkins v. Missouri, 639 F.Supp. 19, 47-48 (W.D. Mo. 
1985), the Court approved a budget for summer school 1986 of 
$1,196,000 which included $200,000 for transportation of a 
projected 3,680 students. The actual enrollment for the summer 
of 1986 program was 2,862. For the summer 
of 1987 the Court 
approved an additional $100,000 for transportation to meet the 
1987 projected enrollment of 3,680 students. The actual enroll- 
ment 
in 1987 
was 3,508. Despite a projected enrollment of 3,200 
students for summer school 1988, the KCMSD requests $353,970 
for transportation costs. It claims that the additional transporta- 
tion funds are needed because in 1987 it was forced to spend 
$100,000 of 
its operating budget on transportation. Because of 

— & 
the projected decrease in enrollment for 1988, the Court simply 
cannot approve an increase in transportation funds over the 
$300,000 alloted for 1987. 
The State argues that the salary component of the summer 
1988 budget is excessive because it is based on pupil/teacher 
ratios of 15 to 1 attheelementary level, 18 to | at the middle level, 
and 22 to 1 for high school summer students, all of which are less 
than the maximum levels set by the Court. 
The Court finds that the lower pupil/teacher ratios on which 
the salary component of the summer school program is based are 
appropriate because the students in the summer program have 
remedial needs that will be better served in smaller classes 
because of the short summer session. For this reason, the Court 
approves the proposed salary component and approves a total 
summer school budget in the amount of $1,249,571 for 1988. 
The Court notes that this funding does not duplicate what was 
purportedly granted by the Desegregation Monitoring Commit- 
tee. On March 31, 1988, the Desegregation Monitoring Com- 
mittee approved the KCMSD’’s request to advance funds neces- 
sary for the implementation of the summer school program for 
1988 from the funding approved for implementation of the seven 
day period in high schools which was not implemented as 
originally scheduled in 1987-88. The KCMSD is directed to 
reimburse the seven day period fund in the total amount ad- 
vanced. 
In its order of July 6, 1987, the Court directed the State to 
include the KCMSD summer school students in its foundation 
formula calculations. 
At the hearing, the State introduced 
evidence that the KCMSD’s total cost for a summer school 
program 
in 1987 was $1,297,415 
and that the KCMSD withdrew 
$945,388 from the State’s program desegregation account for 
1987 summer 
school expenditures. The State also indicated 
that 
the KCMSD would receive an additional $445,748 in foundation 
formula payments 
based upon the 1987 summer school enroll- 
— A-255 — 
ments. The KCMSD has indicated that it will credit the State’s 
payment in the amount of $79,000 resulting in the total amount 
paid or to be paid by the State in desegregation funds and 
foundation formula payments for summer school 1987 of 
$1,312,136, or $14,721 more than the cost of the program. The 
Court had directed the State to include the summer school 
students in its foundation formula because it believed that this 
funding was for summer school expenditures that were 
nondesegregation costs. The KCMSD did operate an elemen- 
tary summer school program prior to the desegregation plan and 
the Court believed that KCMSD’s proposed summer school 
budget included only those expenditures that resulted from the 
desegregation order. However, it appears that the KCMSD has 
submitted its entire summer school costs for 1987 and 1988 as a 
desegregation expense. 
Therefore, unless the KCMSD can 
demonstrate that it incurred summer school costs for its 1987 
program in addition to those included in its 1987 summer schow’ 
budget, then the State has paid for the entire desegregation 
budget for summer school 1987. This was not the intent of the 
Court, and unless the KCMSD can make the showing specified, 
the State is entitled to a credit on its financial obligation for 
summer school 1988 of $340,313. For summer school 1988, the 
State is directed to include the KCMSD summer school students 
in its foundation formula calculations but the payments resulting 
from these additional students are to be applied to the State’s 
desegregation obligation for the 1988 budget. KCMSD argues 
that the summer school payments based upon the foundation 
formula are not categorical payments but rather go into the 
general operating budget of the school district. The Court 
acknowledges this fact but finds that the result is that the State’s 
expenditures for the summer school 1987 exceeded what was 
apparently the total cost of the summer school prograra in 1987. 
Summer School 1989-91 
The KCMSD has proposed a new summer school program for 
summers 1989, 1990, and 1991 that would be fully coordinated 

ha 
— A-256 — 
with the magnet schools. In addition to offering remedial classes 
necessary to help students who are retained in a grade or who are 
falling behind, it would also offer enrichment programs that are 
successful in the magnet schools. 
The KCMSD requests 
$1,793,397 for the summer of 1989, $1,886,663 for the summer 
of 1990, and $1,992,795 for the summer of 1991 to fund these 
new programs. The Court finds that introducing students to the 
magnet courses in these programs will draw new students into 
the magnet schools and offer a much greater desegregation in the 
summer program. However, the Court finds that the budgets 
requested are excessive. Specifically, the Court acknowledges 
that teachers and principals will need to plan for the implemen- 
tation of these summer programs during the preceding school 
year but finds that 120 paid hours per teacher is excessive and the 
Court will approve stipends for 30 hours per teacher. The Court 
also finds that the requested stipends for 200 teachers to partici- 
pate in a staff development program component of the summer 
school program is not a necessary cost and will not be approved. 
The proposed half time coordinator and clerical support to 
coordinate the planning and implementation of the new summer 
school programs are approved. Finally, the Court finds that the 
proposed pupil student ratio of 15 to 1 for remedial classes is 
appropriate and is approved. However, the proposed 20 to | 
pupil-student ratio for enrichment classes is not necessary and 
the Court approves only the cost of teachers and teachers aides 
needed to obtain the maximum class sizes ordered 
by the Court 
for the enrichment component. For these reasons, the Court will 
approve the new summer programs as modified. The proposed 
budget was based on a 20 to | ratio which the Court does not 
approve; therefore, the Court is unable to approve a fixed budget 
because it cannot determine from the submitted budget the exact 
amount of the proposed stipends for planning time and the 
additional cost of teachers and teachers aides needed to obtain 
the maximum pupil teacher ratio for enrichment courses. The 
KCMSD is directed to submit an amended budget reflecting 
— A-257 — 
Full Day Kindergarten 
The KCMSD has requested funding of $1,773,185 for 1988- 
89, $1,862,515 for 1989-90, and $1,956,363 for 1990-91. The 
budget for 1987-88 was $1,826,964. The State argues that the 
KCMSD has not justified the continuation of this program 
because it failed to meet its projected enrollments. The Court 
finds that the KCMSD did fail to meet its projected enrollments 
in 1986-87 and 1987-88, but that the difference between the 
actual and projected figures were relatively small. In 1986-87 
the projected enrollment was 3,482 and the actual enrollment 
was 3,360, a difference of only 120 students. For 1987-88, 
projected enrollment was 3,300 and the actual enrollment was 
3,060, a difference of 240 students. 
Most importantly, the 
evidence presented at the hearing showed that the students 
enrolled in the full day kindergarten for 1985-86 and 1986-87 
scored at or above national norms on the Iowa Test of Basic 
Skills and that these same students continued to score at or above 
the national norms when they were tested at the end of the first 
grade. The full day kindergarten has also had a desegregative 
effect because the percentage of nonminority students partici- 
pating in the program has been above the district wide average. 
This evidence of student achievemnt 
and desegregation warrants 
the continuation of this program. 
For 1988-89, the KCMSD projects that it will need a total of 
171 kindergarten teachers, 15 of which are proposed to be funded 
under the 1986-87 magnet component and five under the base 
budgets component. Therefore, the KCMSD requests funding of 
51 teachers under the full day kindergarten component of the 
desegregation budget based upon a projected enrollment of 
3,160. The KCMSD also requests continuation of the three 
resource teachers, a clerical position to assist in the preparation 
of new materials and to staff the lending library, and workshop 
stipends and related expenses for all kindergarten teachers. 
KCMSD also requests fund for additional supplies in 1988-89, 
1989-90 and 1990-91. 

— A-258 — 
The State claims that the 51 teachers requested to be funded 
under the desegregation budget are excessive because the num- 
ber of teachers should be determined based upon an average 
rather than amaximum 
pupil teacher 
ratio, and because KCMSD’s 
enrollment projections are excessive. The Court has ordered that 
no kindergarten class in the KCMSD shall consist of more than 
22 students and therefore again rejects the State’s argument. 
Regarding the State’s claim of excessive enrollment projection, 
the Court simply cannot determine if KCMSD’s projection is 
accurate. For 1988-89, the Court approves the funding requested 
for teachers, staff and supplies based upon KCMSD’s projection 
but will not approve any increases for services in 1989-90 and 
1990-91 at this time. Furthermore, for 1988-89, the KCMSD is 
ordered to only hire the number of teachers necessary based upon 
the actual enrollment and to only purchase supplies based upon 
that enrollment. The State also claims that during 1987-88 the 
KCMSD hired more teachers than was necessary to serve the 
actual enrollment at the Court ordered levels. The Court finds 
that the State has not presented any evidence supporting this 
claim and will not issue any credit as requested. The State also 
claims that in 1987-88 that the KCMSD paid only 89 rather than 
100 kindergarten teachers out of its operating budget, and that it 
improperly charged the costs of these 11 teachers to the deseg- 
regation budget. Again, based upon the evidence presented at 
the hearing, and the post hearing briefs submitted by the parties, 
the Court finds no evidence to support the State’s claim. How- 
ever, the Court directs the KCMSD to submit documentation to 
the Desegregation Monitoring Committee setting forth the total 
number of kindergarten teachers actually employed by the 
KCMSD for 1987-88 and the number of teachers funded by the 
operating budget and desegregation budget. 
For the reasons stated, the Court approves a fiscal year budget 
of $1,773,185 for 1988-89, 1989-90, and 1990-91. If student 
enrollments justify increased or decreased funding for 1989-90 
and 1990-91, the Court will make an appropriate modification. 
— A-259 — 
Extended Day 
The KCMSD has proposed to reform its before and after 
school program into an extended day program similar to those 
presently offered 
at many of the magnet elementary schools. The 
projected budgets of $1,520,065 for 1988-89, $2,213,083 for 
1989-90, and $3,228,519 for 1990-91, represent large increases 
over the 1987-88 budget of $233,759. 
The new proposals 
include expansion of the number of participating schools from 
the ten to thirteen in 1988-89, to eighteen in 1989-90, and to 
twenty-five in 1990-91. The proposal also increases transporta- 
tion from two afternoons per week to five mornings and five 
afternoons per week. In addition, tutoring would be offered five 
afternoons per week instead of the current two and the enrich- 
ment activities would be greatly expanded. Finally, the program 
calls for a half-time coordinator 
at the central office to coordinate 
the program and a part-time coordinator at each individual 
school to coordinate the implementation of the activities at that 
particular school. 
The State is opposed to continuing the program because it 
claims that it has not achieved its desegregation and student 
achievement goals. In the alternative, the State asserts that even 
if the program is continued for 1989-90, a five-fold increase in 
the budget is not justified. For school year 1985-86, the Court 
approved $104,400 for a before and after school program at ten 
schools. The KCMSD did not implement the plan as ordered and 
the Court did not approve any increase in the budget for 1986-87. 
The program did not meet its projected enrollments for 1986-87 
and in 1987-88 the Court approved an increased budget of $ 
233,759 to provide transportation two afternoons a week and the 
addition of certified teachers to improve the quality of tutoring. 
The Court found that the lack of transportation was the primary 
reason why many students did not participate in the program and 
gave the KCMSD another year to meet its projected enrollments. 
The enrollments 
rose from 398 in 1986-87 
to 462 in the super- 

— A-260 — 
visory component and 527 in the tutorial component in 1987-88. 
The Court also notes that the nonminority enrollment in the 
program also increased from 123 in 1986-87 to 156 in the 
supervisory component and 142 in the tutorial component in 
1987-88. 
Notwithstanding these enrollment increases, the 
KCMSD’s February 19, 1988 Longitudinal Evaluation con- 
cluded that the Before and After School Program “may be 
attracting desegregated enrollments in about half the sites” and 
that the ITBS scores in 1985-86 and 1986-87 “revealed no 
consistently significant differences within grade levels between 
Students who were in the program and random samples of 
nonparticipating students from the same schools.” ITBS scores 
for 1987-88 were not available. The KCMSD acknowledges that 
the program has fallen short of its desegregation and instruc- 
tional goals and proposes an extensive expansion for 1988-89, 
1989-90 and 1990-91. 
Due to the limited success of the program, the Court finds that 
KCMSD’s proposed budget increase for 1989-90 of $1 ,286,306 
is not justified. The Court finds that the increasing enrollments 
is a positive sign and believes that the program should continue. 
The Court will not approve the expansion of the program from 
ten to thirteen schools in 1988-89 but approves an increase in 
tutoring from two to four days per week and also approves an 
increase in transportation from two to five afternoons per week. 
The Court will not approve the half-time coordinator at the 
central office but will approve a part-time coordinator at each 
particular school to implement the programs at those schools. 
The Court finds that a budget of $600,000 is reasonable for this 
program and represents a substantial increase over the preceding 
year. To the extent that the approved budget permits, additional 
enrichment activities are approved. The Court will not at this 
time approve any budget for years 1989-90 and 1990-91. The 
Court will consider budgets for these programs only after an 
evaluation of the success 
of the ITBS scores for 1987-88 
and the 
1988-89 program as approved by the Court. 
— A-261 — 
Early Childhood Education 
The KCMSD requests funding of $4,089,024 for the early 
childhood education program in 1988-89, an increase of ap- 
proximately $900,000 over the 1987-88 budget. KCMSD also 
requests funding of $4,072,395 for 1989-90 and $4,315,207 for 
1990-91. The State argues that the proposed program and budget 
should not be approved because contrary to prior orders of the 
Court, the KCMSD failed to seek input from the State in 
developing the program. In Jenkins v. State of Missouri, 639 
F.Supp. 19, 32 (W.D. Mo. 1985), this Court did direct the State 
of Missouri and the KCMSD to cooperate in irnplementing the 
basic components of the early childhood development pro- 
grams. Despite the State’s claim, the Court v iil consider the 
proposed plan for 1988-89 because of the approaching school 
year. However, the Court will not approve the proposed program 
and budgets 
for 1989-90 and 1990-91, thereby giving 
the parties 
the opportunity to jointly develop an early childhood education 
program for those years. 
The early childhood program consists of three components, 
the early learning centers for three and four year old children, a 
Screening component for one through four year-olds, and a 
parent education program. The early learning centers have becn 
very successful in 1987-88, serving a total of 640 students at 16 
locations. For 1988-89, the KCMSD proposes to expand the 
number of early learning center locations to 25 which would 
allow it to serve a total of 1,000, including approximately 470 
students presently 
on a waiting list. A substantial 
portion of the 
approximate $900,000 increase over last year’s budget consists 
of the additional equipment needed for the nine new learning 
centers and the additional transportation needed for the in- 
creased enrollment. The Court finds that these expenditures 
are 
necessary 
and their cost reasonable. 
The parent education program consists of parents as teachers 
program for families with children from birth to three years of 

— A-262 — 
age and a district wide parent education program for parents of 
children three to four years of age. The parents as teachers 
program provides screening for children ages one and two, 
private visits and group meetings with parents to provide infor- 
mation about normal child growth and educational development 
suggestions, and a lending library with books and toys to be used 
by parents in the program. This program has been very success- 
ful and the Court will approve its continuation at the proposed 
budget. 
To the contrary, the district wide parent education program 
has not been successful. In the fall of 1987, only 45 parents 
participated in the district wide parent education meetings, a 
decrease 
in attendance from both the fall and spring of the 1986- 
87 school year. Despite testimony that 94% of the parents who 
did participate in the program found that their knowledg. 
. 
childhood development had increased because of the progra — 
the Court cannot approve the continuation of the program fo, 
1988-89 because of the lack of participation. The itemized early 
childhood program budget for years 1988-89, 1989-90, and 
1990-91 was admitted at the hearing as State’s Exhibit No. 1. 
However, the Court is unable to ascertain the exact amount 
budgeted for the district wide education program for 1988-89. 
The Court directs the KCMSD to submit this information to the 
Court. 
Screening of children ages one through four is the third 
component of the early childhood development program. The 
State argues that the budget is excessive and should not be 
approved because it estimates that KCMSD’s costs of screening 
three and four year olds 
in 1988-89 will be $222 per child while 
its Cost to screen one and two year olds will only be $15 per child. 
The KCMSD claims that its screening 
costs for three and four 
year olds for 1988-89 is $81.00 per child. Based upon the 
testimony at the hearing, the Court finds that even $81 per 
student is excessive and orders that the KCMSD cost of screen- 
ing 3 and 4 year olds shall not exceed $50 per child. The KCMSD 
— A-263 — 
is further directed to reduce the budget accordingly and submit 
such information to the Court. 
For these reasons, the Court approves a tentative early child- 
hood budget for 1988-89 in the amount of $4,089,024, to be 
modified as found appropriate by the Court. 
Base Budgets 
In its order of July 6, 1987, the Court approved funding of 
$3,171,264 for the base personnel and resources of the two new 
magnets 
that opened in 1987-88, Lincoln South and the New 
Paseo Middle School. The Court found that these costs were 
desegregatica expenses. 
For 1988-89, the KCMSD seeks 
$7,215,392 for continued funding of the base costs of those two 
schools plus the base costs of two elementary magnets that are 
scheduled to open in new facilities in 1988-89, the New Gladstone 
and the New West Elementary 
Schools. The State argues 
that 
these budgets are excessive because they duplicate costs found 
in the AAA budget, the long-range capital improvement plan, 
etc. The Court finds that the State’s position is without merit. 
The overall AAA budget does not include funds for AAA 
materials for the base budgets, and the capital improvements 
expenditures contained in the base budgets are in addition to 
those ordered in the long-range capital improvement plan. The 
Court will not, however, approve the $40,094 proposed for 
facilities maintenance 
in the base budgets. The cost of maintain- 
ing these new facilities is to be financed by the KCMSD from its 
included in the base budgets are reasonable and will approve 
funding for 1988-89 of $7,175,298 for these magnets as deseg- 
regation costs, but will reduce the approved amount by the costs 
avoided elsewhere in the KCMSD because most of the students 
attending these magnets are presently KCMSD students. The 
KCMSD has indicated that it is developing a formula that will 
calculate 
these avoided costs. The Court directs the KCMSD to 
submit the proposed formula and the amount of the costs avoided 

— Sib 
for the school year 1987-38 to the Court by September 30, 1988, 
and for the school year 1988-89 as soon as they are available. 
1986-87 Magnet Schools 
The KCMSD has requested funding of $14,241,784 for the 
operation of original six magnet schools for 1988-89. 
This 
budget represents a 16% increase over the previous order. The 
KCMSD also requests funding of $14,966,072 for 1989-90, and 
$15,870,684 for 1990-91. 
The 1986-87 magnets have been 
successful in terms of actual desegregation and improving stu- 
dent achievement. Lincoln Academy has dropped from 79.1% 
minority in 1985-86 to 64% minority in 1987-88. It has also 
experienced great gains in achievement on the Iowa Test of 
Basic Skills examination, gaining two grades in reading and one 
and a half grades in math in the first year the school 
was a magnet. 
Its students also have a pass rate of 97.1% on the BEST test and 
have won numerous awards and recognitions, including some in 
competitions with suburban districts. 
In 1987-88, Swinney- 
Volker, and the Southwest Cluster magnet schools were all 
within several percentage points of the 60/40 minority/non- 
minority admissions guidelines set by the Court. The children at 
Swinney-Volker and the Southwest Cluster have also achieved 
significant academic gains as they are at or above national norms 
and have won awards in state and regional competitions. In 
1988-89, these six magnet schools will collectively experience 
a 15% enrollment increase over 1987-88. The Court finds that 
this increase justifies a 16% increase 
in the budget. The Court 
also finds that these magnet schools were treated as separate 
student populations for purposes of maintaining AAA levels, 
and therefore that the AAA funds included in the proposed 
budget are not duplicated in the general AAA budget. For these 
reasons, the Court finds that the proposed budget of $14,241,784 
for 1988-89 is reasonable. 
The State argues that only the incremental cost of operating 
these schools as magnets should be approved as desegregation 
— A-265 — 
costs. This would be consistent with the Court-approved long- 
range magnet school budget. In Jenkins v. Missouri, 639 F.Supp. 
19, 53 (W.D. Mo. 1986), the Court found that “magnet schools 
can be utilized to assist in expanding the desegregative educa- 
tional experience for KCMSD students,” and ordered the imple- 
mentation of a fully magnetized program at Lincoln Academy, 
the Southwest Cluster, and Swinney-Volker. The Court ap- 
proved the full cost of the schools in 1986-87 
as a desegregation 
expense. The 1986-87 magnets have proven successful and in 
November of 1986, the Court approved a long-range magnet 
school plan and budget which included only the cost of imple- 
menting the magnet programs at the schools contained within the 
plan, it did not fund the traditional programs at those schools. 
Now that the long-range magnet school plan is underway, the 
Court finds that separate funding treatment of the original 1986- 
87 magnet and those magnets contained within the long-range 
magnet plan is inappropriate. However, the Court is unable 
based upon the evidence before it, to determine these incremen- 
tal magnet costs, and directs the KCMSD to determine the 
portion 
of the 1988-89 proposed budget that consists of operat- 
ing costs which would have been incurred even without the 
Court ordered magnet programs. Because this information 
is not 
available to the Court at this time, the Court will approve the 
proposed 
budget of $14,241,784 for 1988-89 
as a desegregation 
expense, but will allow the State a credit in the amount of these 
incremental costs as approved by the Court against the amount 
of their total desegregation obligation for year 1988-89. The 
Court will not approve the proposed budgets for 1989-90 and 
1990-91 because these figures are also to be revised to reflect 
only the magnet costs of these schools for those years. 
The KCMSD also requests funding of $506,730 for the 
transportation costs of the summer magnet programs at Lincoln 
College Prep, Swinney- Volker, the Southwest Cluster, Franklin, 
Pinkerton, and Knotts Schools for the summer of 1987. The 
Court finds that these are properly desegregation costs but the 

— A-266 — 
Court is unable to determine if the amount requested is reason- 
able. The extended year program was described at the hearing 
but there was no testimony concerning the number of students 
transported. Therefore, the Court will not approve the requested 
amount without additional information. 
AAA 
The KCMSD has requested funding of $6,286,463 to maintain 
its AAA rating in 1988-89, a decrease of $54,151 from 1987-88. 
The KCMSD also seeks funding of $6,483,956 for 1989-90 and 
$6,842,545 for 1990-91. These requested budgets consist of 
salaries for teachers, counselors, librarians and library resources 
to maintain the AAA rating. 
The KCMSD request includes $1,962,153 for library re- 
sources in 1988-89. This amount consists of $143,302 for a 
library resource and distribution center, $285,076 for an audio/ 
visual department, and $1,533,755 for a circulation department, 
of which $1,376,293 is apparently for library resources needed 
at the individual schools to maintain the AAA rating. The Court 
finds that these expenditures are necessary 
and their cost reason- 
able, except for the $1,376,293 in resources contained in the 
circulation department budget. Dr. Casner, KCMSD Director of 
Curriculum testified that he used a per pupil average cost of 
$32.24 to calculate the expenditures required to maintain its 
AAA library resource status, which results in a total cost of 
approximately $1,140,000 for 1988-89. Therefore, the Court 
approves $1,140,000 rather than the proposed 1,376,293 for 
1988-89. The Court makes similar reductions for 1989-90 and 
1990-91. 
The State argues that even $1,140,000 for library resource 
requirements is excessive because KCMSD failed to consider 
those resources purchased with effective schools 
funds and the 
resources contained in the full day kindergarten lending library. 
The Court disagrees. Effective funds were intended to be spent 
— A-267 — 
at each school at the discretion of the school’s principal and 
teacher-parent committees to address specific needs at that 
school. Including library resources purchased with these funds 
for AAA purposes might encourage school officials to purchase 
other items which may not be as needed as additional library 
resources to address a specific educational deficiency. The 
Court also finds that the lending library resources contained in 
the full day kindergarten program are not to be considered for 
AAA purposes because these resources consist of teaching aids 
and kits for teachers in assisting them in instruction, and are not 
resources which directly benefit the students. 
In addition, the KCMSD requests $141,872 for base materials 
and equipment costs for 1988-89. After reviewing the evidence 
presented at the hearing, the Court is unable to determine the 
specific items for which these funds are requested and therefore, 
will not approve these funds at this time. For these reasons, 
the 
Court will approve as a desegregation expense $1,725,860 for 
AAA resources and maintenance in 1988-89, $1,842,048 in 
1989-90, and $1,963,535 for 1990-91. 
The Court will not, 
however, order the State of Missouri defendants to fund any of 
these costs. 
In Jenkins v. Missouri, 639 F.Supp. 19, 47 (W.D. Mo. 1986), 
the Court ordered that KCMSD’ s obligation for library resources 
in 1986-87 of $2,040,936 was “slightly higher than the amount 
needed 
to maintain AAA library resources 
on an ongoing basis” 
and that it “shall be used annually to insure maintenance of AAA 
standard library resources in the remaining years of this deseg- 
regation plan.” With the assistance of the State, the KCMSD 
attained AAA status for library resources in 1986-87 under the 
desegregation 
plan and maintained this rating in 1987-88. Con- 
sistent with the Court’s 
June 16, 1986 order, it is time that the 
KCMSD solely fund the cost of maintaining its library resources 
at the AAA level. 

— A-268 — 
The KCMSD also moves the Court to approve $4,182,438 for 
librarians, counselors, physical education teachers, art teachers, 
music teachers, and substitute teachers needed to maintain the 
AAA status for 1988-89, $4,391,560 for 1989-90, and $4,611,138 
for 1990-91. 
The State argues that the proposed budget is 
excessive because it includes seven additional elementary librar- 
ians, bringing the total number of librarians under the desegre- 
gation budget to 21. The State argues that these seven additional 
librarians exceed AAA requirements. The KCMSD claims that 
the seven librarians will replace 31 planning time aides that were 
funded in previous orders to provide regular teachers with the 
amount of planning time required by AAA. This substitution 
will not only provide the regular teachers with the required 
planning time and reduce the staff cost, but will permit the 
KCMSD to provide a librarian for at least one full day each week 
to all the elementary schools in the district. While this is not an 
AAA requirement, it is an added benefit which results from 
meeting the planning time requirement. The Court approves this 
request. 
The Court also finds that the number of counselors, physical 
education teachers, art teachers, music teachers, and substitute 
teachers requested by the KCMSD are required to maintain the 
AAA rating and approves the proposed budgets. The Court finds 
that these budgets do not duplicate teachers, counselors and 
librarians that are included in the 1986-87 magnets budget and 
base budget. Therefore, the Court approves the proposed bud- 
gets for the costs of teachers, counselors and librarians needed to 
maintain the AAA level for 1988-89, 1989-90, and 1990-91. 
For these reasons, the Court approves an AAA budget of 
$5,908,298 in 1988-89, $1,725,800 for which the KCMSD is 
solely responsible. For 1989-90, the Court approves an AAA 
budget of $6,233,608, with the KCMSD solely responsible for 
$1,842,048. 
For 1990-91, the Court approves a budget of 
$6,754,673, with KCMSD solely responsible for $1,963,535. 
— A-269 — 
Salary Schedule 
For fiscal year 1987-88, the KCMSD had projected an oper- 
ating budget of $121,342,353 and total revenues of $1 10,558,418, 
creating a deficit of $10,783,835. 
Included in the operating 
budget was approximately $7.147 Million for additional teach- 
ers required for the seven period day that was to be implemented 
in the senior high schools beginning 1987-88, and for salary 
increases for both operating and desegregation teachers. In its 
order of September 15, 1987, the Court provided the KCMSD 
with $7.147 Million per fiscal year through 1991-92 to fund the 
teacher salary increases and the additional teachers necessary for 
the seven day period. For 1987-88, the KCMSD granted salary 
increases to its desegregation and nondesegregation teachers out 
of these funds. In the proposed desegregation budgets for 1988- 
89, 1989-90, and 1990-91, the KCMSD includes a 5% per year 
inflation increase for all personnel contained in the budgets, the 
same amount contained in the long-range magnet school budget. 
As a result, the salary increase that the KCMSD granted its 
desegregation teachers out of the $7.147 Million would no 
longer come from those funds, but rather would be funded by the 
desegregation budget. The Court finds that the current salaries 
of these desegregation teachers and any subsequent increases are 
properly desegregation expenses and will approve the 5% per 
year inflation increase contained in all the sub-budgets. How- 
ever, the net result of this modification is that approximately 
$2,000,000 
per year of the $7.147 Million generated 
by the Court 
to fund and maintain 
the 1987-88 salary increases is uncommit- 
ted for fiscal 
year 1988-89 through fiscal year 1991-92. KCMSD 
requests that it be allowed to utilize this fund to grant additional 
salary increases for its teachers who are funded by the operating 
budget. This request will be denied. This Court provided the 
$7.147 Million, part of which was to fund a salary increase for 
nondesegregation teachers, because the Court realized that the 
KCMSD needed additional money to attract and retain high 
quality teachers. However, any further salary increase for the 

— A-270 — 
nondesegregation teachers within the KCMSD will not come 
from desegregation funds but must be granted from KCMSD’s 
operating budget. Furthermore, the Court orders that the uncom- 
mitted $2,000,000 be placed in a reserve because it will be 
needed to fund additional desegregation costs for site selection, 
interest payments, magnet transportation, etc., costs that will 
apparently exceed those originally estimated by the Court. 
PLAINTIFFS’ MOTION TO MODIFY 
DESEGREGATION ORDERS 
Environmenial Science Program 
The KCMSD started its environmental science program at 
North Rock Creek and Korte Elementary Schools in the fall of 
1986, a year prior to the implementation date set forth in the long- 
range magnet plan approved by the Court. 
As a result, the 
KCMSD students who attended these elementary programs and 
who will be entering the sixth grade in the fall of 1988 are without 
an environmental science middle school program since Nowlin 
Middle School is not scheduled to open until the fall of 1989. To 
remedy this problem, the KCMSD requests the Court for a one 
time expenditure of $70,000 to provide a sixth grade environ- 
mental science magnet program at Nowlin School in 1988-89. 
The $70,000 includes $36,000 for a resource teacher to supervise 
the environmental science library, plan and implement field 
trips, and to maintain and dispense equipment. The balance 
consists of $9,000 for field trips, $5,000 for staff development 
and $20,000 for equipment. The Court finds that this program 
is 
necessary to provide continuity to this magnet program. Failure 
to do so may result in parents removing their children from the 
magnet schools and possibly the KCMSD, and cause adverse 
publicity for the magnet program at a time when parental 
confidence in the magnet schools is crucial to its success. 
Therefore, the Court will approve funding of $70,000 for a 
middle school environmental science program at Nowlin School 
for 1988-89. However, the Court finds that certainly some of the 
— A-271 — 
equipment purchased with the $20,000 will not be expended 
during the school year 1988-89 and that the equipment needs for 
the environmental science program at Nowlin School for 1989- 
90 shall be reduced accordingly. 
Central High School 
Central High School is schedule to open its computer magnet 
program 
in 1989 and its classical greek magnet program in 1991- 
92. The computer component will consist of approximately 
1,000 computers and the plaintiffs move the Court to approve 
funding for a system coordinator 
to oversee the computer system 
and a computer technician who will maintain the computers and 
supervise a student repair shop. The cost of these positions will 
be $87,000 per year. 
A computer consultant from Price 
Waterhouse Corporation testified that these positions are neces- 
sary for the computer magnet program to operate and to be cost 
effective. 
The Court agrees and finds that the positions of 
systems coordinator and computer maintenance manager will 
pay for themselves in a relatively short time due to increased 
efficiency, less down time, and lower repair costs. Accordingly, 
the Court will approve these positions at a total cost of $87,000, 
including fringe benefits for 1988-89, with a 5% inflation 
increase per year through 1991-92. 
Physical Education Specialist at 
Northeast Law and Public Service Magnet 
The fire and public safety component of the law and public 
service magnet program at Northeast High School 
is partially 
designed to prepare students to take and pass tests for fire, police, 
ambulance and other similar positions, tests which include 
physical conditioning requirements. The plaintiffs request fund- 
ing of $30,500 per year, including fringe benefits, for an addi- 
tional physical education teacher to address these specific physi- 
cal conditioning requirements. The Court finds that this addi- 
tional physical instruction can be incorporated in the regular 

— A-272 — 
physical education classes presently provided at Northeast and 
thus the Court denies the plaintiffs’ request for an additional 
teacher. 
Additional Technician in the Magnet Personnel Office 
The plaintiffs request funding of $28,000 per year for an 
additional technician to process the applications for staff stipend 
allotments, to recruit applicants for the teacher tuition assistance 
plan, and to obtain visas for the foreign teachers who accept 
employment in the KCMSD as a result of the magnet school 
plan. The manager of employment services for the magnet 
schools stated that these tasks, in addition to her other duties, 
required her to work an average of 14 hours per day. The Court 
finds that an additional technician is necessary and the Court 
approves the position of a personnel technician at a total cost of 
$26,079 for 1988-89, including fringe benefits, with 
a 5% 
inflation increase per year through 1991-92 for a salary adjust- 
ment should the KCMSD chose to grant such increases. 
College Stipend Fund 
The long-range magnet school plan provides for a college 
stipend program by which KCMSD would pay all or part of the 
tuition expenses of full-time undergraduate or graduate students 
in return for a promise by the students to teach in KCMSD 
schools for two or three years upon graduation. The budget for 
this program is $200,000 for 1986-87, $400,000 for 1987-88 and 
1988-89, $300,000 for 1989-90 and $200,000 for 1990-91 and 
1991-92. Due to the timing of the long-range magnet school 
order, the KCMSD claims that none of the $200,000 allocated 
for 1986-87 was expended. KCMSD also states that it will 
expend no more than $25,000 of the budget for the school year 
1987-88. As aresult, the plaintiffs propose that these unexpended 
funds be carried forward and that the budget in the long-range 
plan be modified 
to $0 in 1986-87, $25,000 
in 1987-88, $500,000 
in 1988-89, $400,000 in 1989-90, $400,000 in 1990-91, and 
— A-273 — 
$375,000 in 1991-92, for a total budget of $1,700,000. This 
modification results in no net increase in cost to the parties for 
implementation of this component of the desegregation order. 
The State and the KCMSD are in favor of this modification and 
therefore the Court approves the modification as proposed. 
For the reasons set forth in its order of July 6, 1987 at pp. 13- 
15, the Court orders that the State and the KCMSD are jointly and 
severally liable for the entire budget for 1988-89, except those 
costs that the Court specified are to be borne solely by the 
KCMSD, with the contribution between the two constitutional 
violators to be 75% by the State and 25% by the KCMSD. 
Following are summaries of the 1988-91 desegregation bud- 
gets approved by the Court.
