The Desegregation Monitoring Committee has informed the 
Court of internal disagreements among certain KCMSD board 
members concerning the awarding of jobs and construction 
contracts necessitated 
by the remedial plan to minorities. The 
sole purpose of the Court’s desegregation plan is to eliminate the 
vestiges of the unlawful segregation caused 
by the State of 
Missouri and the KCMSD. Establishment of a quota regarding 

— A-276 — 
the awarding of jobs and contracts to women or minorities is 
beyond the scope of the desegregation plan and will not be 
allowed to interfere with the effective implementation of the 
Court’s remedial orders. The Desegregation Monitoring Com- 
mittee is specifically charged with monitoring this aspect of the 
plan. 
Accordingly, it is hereby 
ORDERED that the Court approves the 1988-89, 1989-90 and 
1990-91 desegregation budgets as set forth in this order; and it is 
further 
ORDERED that the KCMSD is directed to submit to the Court 
modified budgets for year 1988-89 for the early childhood 
education program, the base budgets, and the 1986-87 magnets; 
and it is further 
ORDERED that the KCMSD is to submit amended budgets 
for the 1989, 1990, and 1991 summer school programs. 
/s/ Russell G. Clark 
RUSSELL G. CLARK, 
CHIEF JUDGE 
UNITED STATES 
DISTRICT COURT 
Dated: July 25, 1988 
— A-277 — 
APPENDIX J 
IN THE UNITED STATED DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE WESTERN DISTRICT OF MISSOURI 
WESTERN DIVISION 
No. 77-0420-CV-W-4 
KALIMA JENKINS, et al., 
Plaintiffs, 
vs. 
STATE OF MISSOURL, et al., 
Defendants. 
ORDER 
(Filed: July 5, 1989] 
On May 9 through May 12, 1989, the Court conducted a 
hearing regarding the KCMSD’s motion for approval of a 
desegregation plan for 1989-90. 
Plaintiffs also moved for 
program and budget modifications of prior remedial orders. The 
KCMSD filed a motion to amend its previous motion for ap- 
proval of a desegregation plan for 1989-90. The KCMSD’s 
motion to amend will be granted. In addition, 
the parties filed 
post-hearing briefs in support of and in opposition to the 
KCMSD’s and plaintiffs’ motions. Based upon the evidence 
presented at the hearing and the filings with the Court, the Court 
will grant the KCMSD’s motion for approval of a desegregation 
plan for 1989-90 
and plaintiffs’ motion for program and budget 
modifications of prior remedial orders as set forth in this order. 
The State filed a motion to strike plaintiffs’ reply brief. Plaintiffs 
filed suggestions in opposition to the State’s motion to strike. 
The State’s motion to strike will be denied. The Court will make 
DMC appointments 
for two year terms beginning July 1, 1989, 
as set forth in this order. 

— A-278 — 
KCMSD’S MOTION FOR APPROVAL OF A 
DESEGREGATION PLAN FOR 1989-90 
AAA 
In its order of July 25, 1988, this Court approved a 1989-90 
AAA budget of $6,233,608 with the KCMSD solely responsible 
for $1,842,048 for AAA resources and maintenance. Jenkins v. 
State of Missouri, Order of July 25, 1988, at 21-24 (W.D. Mo. 
1988). The KCMSD now proposes two modifications in the 
AAA component for 1988-89 resulting in a slightly reduced 
budget request of $6,231,123. First, the KCMSD requests that 
one of the AAA librarians originally approved at the high school 
level be allocated among the four special schools because such 
schools previously have not had the services of a librarian. The 
KCMSD states that based on high school enrollment projections 
for 1989-90, the KCMSD will need only 12 of the 13 Court- 
approved high school librarians in order to retain AAA status. A 
June 17, 1988, letter from the State informing the KCMSD that 
it had received an AAA rating for 1987-88 contained the follow- 
ing: 
ADVISEMENTS: 
The following advisements relate to 
standards which are not being fully met for the specific 
classification rating. These items should be corrected and 
fully met before the next school year opens unless a previ- 
ous agreement for progressively planned improvement has 
been made with the Supervisor of Instruction: 
* 
* 
* 
V-3 The services of a qualified learning resource director 
or librarian should be provided at least one-fourth time 
in the high school. (Fairview Alternative) 
KCMSD Exhibit 2 at 2. A May 19, 1989, letter from the State 
informing the KCMSD that it had received an AAA rating for 
1988-89 contained the following advisement: “Support services 
— A-279 — 
at all special and alternative schools should be provided.” 
KCMSD’s Reply to State and AFT 691’s Post-Hearing Memo- 
randa Regarding Year 5 Desegregation Budget Proposal, Ex- 
hibit 
A at 2. As a result, the Court will approve the allocation of 
one of the AAA high school librarians among the four special 
schools. 
The KCMSD’s second proposed modification is a 
minor reduction in the AAA budget for library resources due to 
a refinement in the mathematical calculations. The Court will 
approve such reduction. Therefore, the Court will approve an 
AAA budget of $6,231,123 in 1989-90 with the KCMSD solely 
responsible for $1,842,048 for AAA resources and maintenance. 
1986-87 Magnet Schools 
In its order of July 25, 1988, the Court approved a 1988-89 
budget of $14,241,784 for the 1986-87 magnets but stated that it 
would allow the State a credit for base costs necessary to operate 
a traditional program at each school. Jenkins, Order of July 25, 
1988, at 19-21. The Court also refused to approve a proposed 
budget of $14,966,072 for 1989-90 “because these figures are 
also to be revised to reflect only the magnet costs of these schools 
for those years.” Id. at21. The KCMSD subsequently submitted 
a budget for 1988-89 delineating between base operating costs 
and incremental 
magnet costs. The Court refused to approve the 
budgets “without further justification that the items identified by 
the State in its response are properly allocated 100% 
to theme 
costs.” Jenkins, Order of April 3, 1988, at 5. The KCMSD’s 
budget request for the 1986-87 magnets for 1989-90 is 
$14,805,851, slightly lower than the previous request of 
$14,966,072 for 1989-90 
due to an anticipated enrollment reduc- 
tion at Hartman and Swinney and a refinement in the mathemati- 
cal calculations for AAA library resources. 
The State argues that the 1986-87 magnet budget components 
should not be addressed by the Court until the KCMSD has 
satisfied this Court’s order of April 3, 1989. The KCMSD 
counters that the only issue is whether the 1989-90 budget is 

— A-280 — 
reasonable and that the KCMSD will comply with the Court’s 
April 3, 1989, order in a separate brief. This Court has previously 
found that the 1986-87 magnets have been successful in deseg- 
regating and improving student achievement. Jenkins, Order of 
July 25, 1988, at 19. Therefore, the Court finds that the proposed 
budget of $14,805,851 for 1988-89 is reasonable and will ap- 
prove such budget as a desegregation expense, but will allow the 
State a credit in the amount of the base costs necessary to operate 
a traditional program at each school against the amount of their 
total desegregation obligation for 1989-90. The Court directs the 
KCMSD to submit budgets for the 1986-87 magnets for 1988-89 
and 1989-90 delineating between base operating costs and 
incremental magnet costs with appropriate justification for items 
allocated 100% to theme costs as required by the Court’s order 
of April 3, 1989, on or before August 31, 1989. Therefore, the 
Court will not approve the KCMSD’s proposed 1990-91 budget 
of $15,706,549 at this time. 
Effective Schools 
The Court approved an effective schools budget of $7,300,000 
for 1989-90 in its order of July 25, 1988. Jd. at7. The KCMSD 
now requests a $275,000 reduction in the previously approved 
budget resulting from changes in projections of minority enroll- 
ment and program modifications. The State does not challenge 
the KCMSD’s proposal. Therefore, the KCMSD’s proposed 
budget for 1989-90 for effective schools of $7,025,000 will be 
approved.

for 1989-90. The State does not oppose this proposal. The Court 
will approve the base budget component of $9,594,079 as a 
desegregation expense. However, the State will be allowed a 
credit for the amount of costs avoided elsewhere as addressed by 
the Court in its order of May 24, 1989. The Court directs 
the 
— A-281 — 
KCMSD to submit the amount of costs avoided elsewhere for 
1988-89 to the Court on or before August 31, 1989 and for 1989- 
90 as soon as they are available. 
Early Childhood Education 
Pursuant to this Court’s order of July 25, 1988, the KCMSD 
and the State have cooperatively developed the 1989-90 early 
childhood education program. See id. at 16-17. The result is a 
proposed 1989-90 budget of $3,607,545, which is $481,479 less 
than the 1988-89 budget approved by the Court. See id. at 18. 
The Court will approve the 1989-90 budget of $3,607,545. 
Extended Day 
The Court previously approved a $600,000 extended day 
budget for ten traditional schools for 1988-89, but refused to 
approve budgets for 1989-90 and 1990-91 stating 
that the Court 
would consider those budgets “only after an evaluation of the 
success of the ITBS scores for 1987-88 and the 1988-89 program 
as approved by the Court.” Id. at 16. For 1989-90 the KCMSD 
proposes that the extended day program continue at six of the ten 
schools where it is currently operating and requests a budget of 
$405,000. 
The State argues that the KCMSD has failed to 
present evidence regarding the success of the program as re- 
quired by this Court’s order of July 25, 1988. 
Testimony at the hearing overwhelmingly indicated that par- 
ents and teachers favor the extended day program and that the 
program teaches children social skills, enhances desegregation 
and increases academic performance. In 1988-89 39% of the 
students in before school programs, 37% of the students 
in after 
school supervision programs and 34% of the students in after 
school tutoring programs were nonminority students. KCMSD 
Exhibit 1 at § 4 p.3. These figures compare with 35%, 35% and 
34%, respectively, for 1987-88. Id. The total number of 
students 
enrolled in the after school supervision and tutoring programs 

— A-282 — 
has increased dramatically from 1987-88 to 1988-89, while 
enrollment in the before school program has remained almost 
constant. Jd. Overall, total participation by nonminority students 
in the extended day program has increased from 33% in 1987-88 
to 37% in 1988-89, while enrollment increased from 681 stu- 
dents in 1987-88 to 1,217 students in 1988-89. State’s Exhibit 
7 at 2. ITBS scores for 1985-86, 1986-87 and 1987-88 indicate 
a consistent trend of increased performance for students in the 
extended day program. KCMSD Exhibit | at § 4 p.5. Addition- 
ally, students participating in the 1987-88 extended day program 
consistently outperformed nonparticipants on the ITBS. 
Id. 
Achievement data for 1988-89 is not yet available because the 
achievement tests were not administered until April of 1989. 
(Tr. at 242). Thus, the KCMSD has presented evidence regard- 
ing the success of the program as required by this Court’s order 
of July 25, 1988, and the Court finds that the evidence justifies 
the continuation of the extended day program at the six tradi- 
tional schools. 
The State argues that a $405,000 budget request represents a 
12.5% increase in the average per school budget for the program. 
The KCMSD’s request consists of $250,000 for salaries, $85,000 
for transportation and $70,000 for materials and supplies. 
KCMSD Exhibit | at § 4 p. 4. Salary costs were derived based 
upon the 1988-89 extended day enrollment at the six schools 
remaining in the program and include funding for the part-time 
extended day facilitator at each school. Jd. Transportation costs 
were determined based upon the number of buses each school 
used for afternoon transportation five days a week in 1988-89. 
Id. Costs for materials and supplies were based upon projected 
enrollments for 1989-90 and expenditures of the six schools for 
1988-89. 
Id. The Court finds that the extended day budget 
request for $405,000 is reasonable and, therefore, will approve 
such request. 
— A-283 — 
Full Day Kindergarten 
The Court previously approved a full day kindergarten budget 
of $1,773,185 for 1989-90 stating that “[i]f student enrollments 
justify increased 
or decreased funding for 1989-90... , the Court 
will make an appropriate modification.” Jenkins, Order of July 
25, 1988, at 14. The KCMSD has not requested a modification 
of the previously approved budget for 1989-90 and believes that, 
based on enrollment projections, the budget is sufficient. The 
State argues that the KCMSD has not demonstrated that the 
previously approved budget is necessary to serve the number of 
students currently projected for 1988-89. The Court does not 
find it necessary to modify the previously approved full day 
kindergarten budget of $1,773,185 for 1989-90. 
Reduction in Class Size 
The Court previously approved a reduction in class size 
budget of $8,787,191 for 1989-90 stating that “[i]f student 
enrollments justify additional funding for 1989-90... , KCMSD 
may move the Court for such funding.” Jd. at 8. The KCMSD 
did not request a modification in the previously approved reduc- 
tion in class size budget. The State argues that the budget is 
excessive based on projected enrollments and that the State has 
long since exceeded the $12,000,000 funding obligation cap 
previously set forth by the Court. See Jenkins, 639 F.Supp. 19, 
30 (W.D. Mo. 1985). 
In its order 
of July 25, 1988, the Court placed a funding 
obligation on the State for 75% of the $8,787,191 class size 
reduction budget for 1988-89, which caused the State 
to exceed 
the $12,000,000 
funding cap previously set forth by the Court. 
See Jenkins, 
Order of July 25,1988, 
at 7-9. The Court listed three 
reasons for allowing the funding cap to be exceeded in 1988-89: 
(1) maximum class sizes were not attained in 1987-88; 
(2) at the 
time 
of the 1985 order, the KCMSD was not operating middle 
schools nor did its high schools have a seven period day; and (3) 

— A-284 — 
maximum class sizes must be maintained once achieved and 
nothing in the 1985 order precluded the Court from ordering the 
State to fund a share of the maintenance. Jd. at 8. Although no 
evidence was presented at the hearing regarding the reduction in 
class size budget, the State claims that maximum class sizes were 
attained in 1988-89, that maximum class sizes were reached in 
the context of full implementation of middle schools and the 
seven period days and, finally, that the Court has previously 
recognized that maintenance is a KCMSD obligation. State’s 
Post Hearing Memorandum Regarding Year 5 Desegregation 
Budget Proposal at 11 n.9. The KCMSD did not refute the 
State’s claims except to quote from the Court’s July 25, 1988, 
order that the 1985 order does not preclude the Court from 
ordering the State to fund a share of the maintenance for class 
size reduction. KCMSD’s Reply to State and AFT 691’s Post- 
Hearing Memoranda Regarding Year 5 Desegregation Budget 
Proposal at 4. 
The Court recognizes that the State’s original funding cap of 
$12,000,000 has been exceeded. Nonetheless, the Court im- 
posed the $12,000,000 funding cap and, therefore, the Court has 
the discretion to increase the cap or to require State funding 
to 
further exceed the cap. 
Although the Court has previously 
recognized that it is not precluded from ordering the State to fund 
a share of the maintenance for class size reduction, 
the Court has 
also previously recognized that the KCMSD should be respon- 
sible for maintenance under the plan. See Jenkins, Order of July 
25,1988, at 19; Jenkins, 639 F.Supp. at 41. The Court previously 
stated: “While 
the funds allocated by this Court for the purpose 
of reducing class size will provide significant assistance, the 
financial responsibility for reaching beyond these minimal goals 
and reaching toward educational excellence must be borne by the 
patrons 
of the KCMSD.” Id. at 30. The Court believes it is time 
for the KCMSD to begin to bear the responsibility for mainte- 
nance of class size reduction goals achieved under the plan. 
Therefore, the Court will gradually increase the KCMSD’s 
— A-285 — 
funding responsibility for the class size reduction budget. The 
KCMSD will be responsible for 50% of the 1989-90 budget of 
$8,787,191, 75% of 
the 1990-91 budget and 100% of the 1991- 
92 budget. The Court realizes that the KCMSD may not have 
adequate desegregation funds to satisfy this funding obligation 
in total. 
To insure that funds are available for class size 
reduction, the State will be required to make up any shortfall in 
the KCMSD’s funding obligation pursuant to joint and several 
liability and the revised drawdown procedure previously ap- 
proved by the Court. See Jenkins, Order of January 3, 1989, at 
4-5. However, for purposes of indemnity, the funding obligation 
is placed 50% on the KCMSD for 1989-90.

The State argues that the 1989-90 
funding level should be $1,444. State’s Post Hearing Memoran- 
dum Regarding Year 5 Desegregation Budget Proposal at 16. 
The Court will adopt the analysis set forth in its order of July 25, 
1988: “The Court finds that these expenditures are necessary 
and their cost reasonable and approves them as part of the 
a? budget for [1989-90]. Jenkins, Order of July 25, 
, at 
2.

Debt Service 
The KCMSD requests that the Court recognize as a desegre- 
gation expense the KCMSD’s full cost of debt service of the 
$10,000,000 bond issue for the KCMSD’s share of the initial 
$37,000,000 in Court-ordered capital improvements. 
The 
KCMSD argues that the principal payments, like the interest 
payments, are a part of the KCMSD’s cost of obtaining its share 
of funding for Court-ordered capital improvements under the 
remedial plan and, therefore, are proper desegregation expendi- 
tures. The State opposes the KCMSD’s request. The Court will 
deny the KCMSD’s request. The KCMSD has previously been 
given credit against its capital improvement obligations for the 
$10,000,000 bond issue principal. See Jenkins, 639 F.Supp. at 
40-41. 
Latin Grammer Magnets 
The KCMSD seeks funding to provide two new uniforms for 
each student in the elementary Latin Grammer magnets and to 
provide periodic replacement of worn out and outgrown uni- 
forms. The KCMSD requests a uniform budget of $47,680 for 
1989-90, $39,360 for 1990-91 and $39,360 for 1991-92. The 
State argues that “it is not enough that the State is funding the 
education of KCMSD students; it now demands that the State 
clothe the students as wel.” Response of State Defendants to 
KCMSD Motion for Approval of Desegregation Plan 1989-90 
and to Motion of Plaintiffs for Program and Budget Modifica- 
tions of Prior Remedial Orders at 3. 
The Long Range Magnet Plan (“LRMP”’) provides that “stu- 
dents will be required to wear a school uniform” at Latin 
— A-287 — 
Grammer elementary magnets and that “[uJniforms will be 
provided by the District.” LRMP at 117. Article IX, § 1(a) of the 
Missouri Constitution requires public schools to furnish “gratu- 
itous instruction.” The State interprets this constitutional provi- 
sion to prohibit school districts from charging a fee for any aspect 
of a public education that is required 
by a district, (Tr. at 829-30), 
such as uniforms. Additionally, Ms. Marie Grant, the principal 
at Pinkerton Latin Grammer elementary magnet, testified that 
she could see the difference in the behavior of the students due 
to the uniforms, stating that the students were “more structured, 
and more ladylike and manlylike in their uniforms.” (Tr. at 138). 
She also testified that parents like the uniforms because there is 
no competition among students with regard to clothing. (Tr. at 
143). The Court will approve a 1989-90 budget of $47,680 for 
uniforms at the Latin Grammer elementary magnets and 1990- 
91 and 1991-92 uniform budgets of $39,360. 
The KCMSD also seeks accelerated and increased funding for 
computers at the Latin Grammer elementary magnets. 
The 
KCMSD requests a 1989-90 budget of $173,700. The KCMSD 
states that budget lines for computers in the LRMP provide for 
25 computers at Pinkerton and 20 computers at Garfield, but that 
these numbers are insufficient to meet the LRMP requirement of 
4 computers in each classroom at the schools. The KCMSD 
states that 35 more computers are needed at Pinkerton and 64 at 
Garfield. Also included within the budget request are funds for 
networking 
computers with a computer lab. The State counters 
that the LRMP took into account computers that were already in 
use at each school or that were to be provided through other 
funding sources and, therefore, the funding provided under the 
LRMP is appropriate. 
The LRMP states that “[eJach classroom will house four 
microcomputers for individualized and/or networked courses.” 
LRMP 
at 118. Pinkerton 
has 15 classrooms. Thus, 
to satisfy the 
LRMP Pinkerton needs 60 computers. 
The LRMP budget 

— A-288 — 
provides funds for 25 computers, leaving a need for 64 comput- 
ers. The Long Range Capital Improvements Plan (“LRCIP”) 
calls for a computer lab at the Latin Grammer elementary 
magnets. Additional computers purchased with other funds for 
use in the labs should not be counted against the LRMP computer 
requirements. 
See Jenkins, Order of July 25, 1988, at 22. 
Testimony at the hearing indicated that these computers should 
be made available as soon as possible for one-on-one and group 
instruction of students on basics. (Tr. at 153-54). The Court will 
approve the 1989-90 budget request of $173,700 for computers 
at Latin Grammer elementary magnets. 
Visual and Performing Arts Magnets 
The KCMSD requests funding for additional art and music 
teachers and two Suzuki violin teachers at the Visual and 
Performing Arts elementary magnets in the amount of $174,394 
for 1989-90, $183,114 for 1990-91, and $192,769 for 1991-92. 
These magnets are to emphasize the arts areas of music, art, 
theater and dance. The LRMP budget funds two arts teacher 
positions at each magnet and the site task forces decided that 
since music and art were currently offered by the KCMSD, the 
two arts teacher positions should be designated as theater and 
dance. The KCMSD argues that the additional music and arts 
teacher positions are needed to make the arts and music compo- 
nent of the magnets different from what is offered in the rest of 
the KCMSD. 
The State counters that the LRMP does not 
envision four separate teachers at the elementary level to teach 
art, music, theater and dance, but instead contemplates arts 
teachers to cover the whole range of the curriculum. 
The LRMP provides that elementary classes grades K-2 will 
participate “in daily arts projects presented by the primary level 
arts teacher. Projects are designed for student exploration in 
dance, theater, music, and art. The homeroom teacher 
and art 
teacher will integrate the skills, concepts, and understandings of 
the core subjects 
and the arts projects.” LRMP 
at 89-90 (empha- 
— A-289 — 
sis added). Elementary students in grades 3-5 will be allowed to 
schedule “aesthetic” time daily allowing freedom to explore 
dance, theater, 
music and art. Jd. at 90. Thus, it appears 
that the 
State’s interpretation of the LRMP is correct; the LRMP does not 
envision four separate teachers at the elementary level to teach 
the four areas of art,music, dance and theater, but instead 
contemplates arts teachers to cover the range of 
the curriculum. 
This interpretation is supported by the LRMP’s requirement for 
middle schools that “[eJach student’s schedule reflects arts time 
daily, much the same 
as the intermediate unit, 
but the instructor 
is always an arts specialist.” 
Id. (emphasis added). 
The 
KCMSD argues that students at the Visual and Performing Arts 
elementary magnets currently only receive one hour of art and 
one hour of music instruction per week, which provides only the 
most basic program. However, testimony at the hearing indi- 
cated that elementary students only receive one hour of drama 
and one hour of dance instruction per week even with the two 
LRMP arts teacher positions designated as theater and dance. 
(Tr. at 
531). This was in addition to playing a musical instrument 
two hours per week. (Tr. at 528, 529). Thus, the Court is not 
convinced that the additional art and music teachers are neces- 
sary under the LRMP and will not approve such teachers. 
The KCMSD also requests funds for two Suzuki violin teaches 
for Suzuki classes required to be offered as an elective by the 
LRMP. The KCMSD argues that Suzuki instruction resulted in 
more student/parent interest in the program than could be ac- 
commodated, with a waiting list at all {our elementary schools. 
The State counters that the KCMSD has not demonstrated a need 
for additional desegregation 
funding for Suzuki teachers 
and that 
Suzuki is simply an elective for students with no requirement 
‘hat 
every student expressing an 
interest be 
given achance totake 
It. 
The LRMP provides that Suzuki violin be offered as an 
elective at the elementary 
magnets. LRMP 
at 90. During the 

— A-290 — 
1988-89 academic year ene Suzuki teacher visited each of the 
four magnets every day because the Suzuki method requires 
multiple weekly sessions with parents and students. Suzuki is 
attractive to parents, requires parent participation and induces a 
higher level of thinking that carries over into the classroom. (Tr. 
at 472-73, 524-25). There is a waiting list for Suzuki at all four 
Visual and Performing Arts elementary magnets and parents 
have less interest in enrolling students in the magnets when 
Suzuki classes 
are filled. (Tr. at 
474-75). The Court will approve 
the funding of two Suzuki teachers under the LRMP, as Suzuki 
provides desegregative attractiveness to the elementary Visual 
and Performing Arts magnets. However, because the KCMSD 
combined its budget request for Suzuki teachers with the request 
for art and music teachers, the Court cannot ascertain the budget 
necessary for the Suzuki teachers. The KCMSD is directed to 
submit a budget request for the certified Suzuki teachers for 
1989-90 on or before August 31, 1989. 
Defensive Driving Program 
The KCMSD requests funding of $55,883 for 1989-90, $2,830 
for 1990-91 and $2,830 for 1991-92 for a defensive driving 
program in the Law, Public Service and Military magnet at 
Northeast High School. The KCMSD argues that the program 
is 
designed to support the magnet themes at Northeast High School 
and will provide career exploration in law enforcement and 
public service. The State argues that the program is misguided 
and bears no relationship to law and public service. 
The KCMSD’s proposal states that the purpose of the course 
is “to familiarize students with the proper procedures for oper- 
ating and maintaining emergency vehicles and public safety 
vehicles used in the metropolitan area and to assist students in 
developing minimum skills necessary to obtain jobs within the 
law and public service field and the military.” KCMSD Exhibit 
1 at § 11 p. 12. However, testimony at the hearing indicated that 
defensive driving skills are not relevant to hiring decisions in the 
— A-291 — 
law and public service areas. (Tr. at 713, 726-27, 752, 755-56, 
772-74). Such skills are taught to new recruits in the law and 
public service areas even if the recruit has previously had such 
training. (Tr. at 714-15, 728, 774). Although the defensive 
driving program contains a vehicle maintenance component, 
law and public service agencies do not teach maintenance to 
recruits, but instead send the vehicles to a maintenance center for 
care. (Tr. at 717, 729-30). Witnesses agreed that they would be 
against the teaching of defensive driving to high school students. 
(Tr. at 718, 753-54). Additionally, safety of the students is a 
concern. (Tr. at 756). Based on the evidence introduced at the 
hearing, the Court cannot approve the defensive driving pro- 
gram. 
Magnet Extended Day 
The KCMSD requests budgets of $500,000 for 1989-90, 
1990-91, and 1991-92 for extended day programs at Pinkerton, 
Knotts, Attucks, Garfield 
and Troost magnets. The State argues 
that the KCMSD has failed to present evidence regarding the 
success of the program as required by this Court’s order of July 
25, 1988. Additionally, the State argues that the KCMSD’s 
request is inappropriate 
under the LRMP because it would place 
an extended day program at every elementary magnet school. 
The Court addressed the State’s argument regarding evidence 
on the success of the extended day program previously in this 
order and determined that the KCMSD had presented evidence 
as required by this Court’s order of July 25, 1988. See supra 
pp. 
5-6. However, the LRMP provides that “/sJome elementary 
magnets will offer an extended day program.” LRMP 
at 56 
(emphasis added). The State argues that the KCMSD requested 
funding for extended day programs at all schools becoming 
magnets in 1989-90 as well as at each of the elementary schools 
proposed for new themes, and that when this request is combined 
with extended day funding already provided under the LRMP, 
there will be an extended day program at every elementary 

— A-292 — 
magnet school in contravention of the LRMP. The KCMSD 
previously requested funding for extended day programs at 
Knotts and Pinkerton. The Court responded: 
First, the long-range magnet plan proposed by the 
KCMSD and approved by this Court does not provide 
an extended day program at Knotts and Pinkerton 
Elementary Schools. The Court finds, as the drafters 
of the long-range magnet plan certainly believe, that it 
is the specific magnet programs at these schools that 
must attract the non-minority students. Furthermore, 
due to the limited success of the extended day pro- 
gram, the Court cannot rely on it to desegregate the 
KCMSD and therefore will deny the KCMSD’s re- 
quest for additional funding for extended day pro- 
grams at Knotts and Pinkerton at this time. 
Jenkins, Order of August 29, 1988, at 3. Once again, the Court 
adopts this analysis and will not approve the KCMSD’s request 
forextended day programs at Pinkerton, Knotts, Attucks, Garfield 
and Troost magnets at this time. 
AVATS 
The KCMSD requests that the Court approve deferring the 
opening of the magnet program at the Advanced Vocational and 
Techinical High School (“AVATS”) from 1989-90 to 1990-91. 
The KCMSD states that the AVATS building needs to be 
vacated for 1989-90 to permit additions and renovations and that 
the program cannot begin in an annex due to the difficulty and 
cost involved in purchasing equipment, installing it in the annex 
and then returning it to its permanent location. The KCMSD also 
requests the following budget realignment resulting in no cost 
increase for the program: $3,164,068 for 1989-90, $3,079,907 
for 1990-91, and $2,673,070 for 1991-92. The State argues that 
the KCMSD proposed the AVATS renovations nearly three 
years ago but failed to explain why the capital improvements 
are 
— A-293 — 
not yet underway, that the AVATS site task force has been in 
place for nearly two years but a curriculum has not been devel- 
oped and that no principal has been hired. 
Mr. Willy Giles, assistant superintendent supervising the 
planning 
of the AVATS magnet program, testified 
that there was 
no way the AVATS facility would be ready for students by 
September of 1989. (Tr. at 66). Thus, the Court has no choice 
but to grant the KCMSD’s request for deferral of the opening of 
the AVATS magnet school, as denial of the request would cause 
the magnet program and the students to suffer. However, the 
tragic result 
of the situation is that the children who are students 
in the KCMSD suffer the most as a result of the KCMSD’s poor 
planning process. The Court warns that those persons respon- 
sible for the AVATS planning process may be held in contempt 
of Court if the planning process for the AVATS is not improved 
and the AVATS magnet program is not implemented 
as planned 
in 1990-91. Additionally, the Court warns that if proper plan- 
ning does not occur, desegregation funds will not be approved 
for the AVATS. With this in mind, the Court will approve the 
deferral of the opening of the AVATS and the realigned budget 
as proposed by the KCMSD. 
Music Accompanists 
The KCMSD seeks funding for three music accompanists at

posed 
by the KCMSD. 

_— 
Unthemed Magnets 
In phases VI and VII of the LRCIP there are three elementary 
schools and two middle schools for which no themes have been 
provided. The KCMSD proposes that the elementary schools be 
themed as Latin Grammer (“Elementary I’), Reading and Lan- 
guage Arts Academy (“Elementary IT”), and Environmental 
Science (“Elementary IIT’), and that the middle schools be 
themed as an Arts and Science Academy (“Middle I’) and 
Classical Greek (“Middle IT’), with the Classical Greek theme 
being shifted from Northeast Middle School to Middle I. The 
KCMSD proposes that Northeast Middle be themed as Global 
Studies. 
Finally, the KCMSD requests that the Gifted and 
Talented theme of Southeast Elementary III be deferred to a later 
phase to permit the KCMSD to review the overall gifted and 
talented program, with Southeast III being themed a Montessori 
magnet. To provide these themes, the KCMSD requests a 1989- 
90 budget of $1,886,040, a 1990-91 budget of $3,050,947 and a 
1991-92 budget of $3,022,064. 
The State argues that Elementary I, II and III were proposed 
by the KCMSD and ordered by the Court under the LRCIP as 
traditional schools, not magnet schools. The State argues that a 
three page chart setting forth proposed funding allocations the 
KCMSD submitted to the Court in conjunction with its Motion 
for Approval of the Long Range Capital Improvements Plan 
indicates that Elementary I, [I and III were ordered as traditional 
schools. See States Post Hearing Memorandum Regarding Year 
5 Desegregation Budget Proposal at Attachment 5. The State 
argues that the chart indicates that the three schools were not 
designated as magnets and that the proposed 75/25 allocation 
for 
the schools on the chart is consistent with the allocation proposed 
by the KCMSD for nonmagnet work. /d. at 18 n.18. The State 
concludes that maintaining these schools as traditional schools 
is consistent with the LRMP directive that half of the KCMSD’s 
elementary schools remain as traditional schools. The KCMSD 
— A-295 — 
did not refute the State‘s argument, but instead stated that “[t)he 
more serious flaw with the State’s argument is that it fails to 
explain how these schools will draw desegregated enrollments 
as traditional schools.” KCMSD’s Reply 
to State and AFT 691’s 
Post-Hearing memoranda Regarding Year 5 Desegregation 
Budget Proposal at 11. 
The LRMP provides that while every senior high and middle 
school will become a magnet school, “[a]pproximately half of 
the elementary schools either will become complete magnets or 
will have a magnet them.” LRMP at 1. The Court is not 
convinced that Elementary I, II and III were ordered as magnet 
schools and, therefore, will not approve the KCMSD’s request to 
provide magnet themes for these schools at this time. However, 
the Court will request that the parties file further briefs on the 
issue of whether Elementary I, II and II] were ordered as magnet 
schools under the LRMP 
or the LRCIP 
on or before August 31, 
1989. 
The State opposes the KCMSD proposal for an Arts and 
Sciences magnet theme at Middle I arguing that the desegregative 
attractiveness of the theme has not been established and that the 
theme describes an ordinary middle school. Dr. Ronald Simpson, 
director of school management for the KCMSD, testified that the 
theme is designed “to create an exceptionally good liberal arts 
middle school that has a humanities orientation that tends to take 
the middle school child and teach that student the things that we 
think they need to teach the way they learn, not particularly the 
way it is convenient for us to teach.” (Tr. at 347). A member of 
the SAC for Middle I testified that elementary students in the 
Applied Learning Center magnets had no middle schools in 
which to enroll that were multi-disciplinary with broad liberal 
arts concepts. (Tr. at 430-31). Middle I is designed to be a feeder 
school into the Visual and Performing Arts High School, the 
engineering program at Van Horn High School, and the South- 
west Math and Science High School. (Tr. 
at 350). Although Dr. 

— A-296 — 
Terry Stewart testified that the Arts and Science Academy was 
a “common, ordinary, good, sound, basic middle school” with a 
new approach to the government of the school, (Tr. at 792,793), 
the Court finds that the Arts and Science Academy is an appro- 
priate magnet theme. The theme uses a unique teacher center 
concept and will provide students an in depth arts and sciences 
curriculum. See KCMSD Exhibit | at § 11 pp. 32-33. The Court 
will approve the Middle I Arts and Sciences theme and will also 
approve the proposed budgets of $537, 184 for 1989-90, $890,690 
for 1990-91 and $852,350 for 1991-92. 
The State argues that Court approval of moving the middle 
school Classical Greek theme from Northeast Middle School to 
Middle II at the Jewish Community Center should be withheld 
pending KCMSD’s elaboration on the necessary additions, 
renovations and costs. Although the Jewish Community Center 
would require adaptations and addition of classrooms, it would 
be much more cost effective to implement the Classical Greek 
theme there than at Northeast. (Tr. at 427-28); KCMSD Exhibit 
1 at § 11 p. 35. Locating the program at Northeast would require 
the acquisition of two city blocks currently containing housing 
and a school, while locating the program at the Jewish Commu- 
nity Center would require no property acquisition. (Tr. at 352- 
53). 
Additionally, the Northeast Middle SAC felt that the 
Classical Greek theme was inappropriate for the area due to the 
necessity of land acquisition. (Tr. at 438). Thus, the Court will 
approve the shifting of the Classical Greek theme from North- 
east Middle School to Middle II. 
The State opposes the KCMSD’s proposed Global Studies 
theme at Northeast Middle by arguing that the proposal is vague 
and poorly planned. The Global Studies them is based upon 
social studies with a strong foreign language component. (Tr. at 
355). Students can also place an emphasis on governmental 
studies and geography. KCMSD Exhibit 1 at § 11 p. 37. This 
theme will allow students from the elementary foreign language 
— A-297 — 
program to exploit the fact that they do know a foreign language. 
(Tr. at 356-57, 449). As there are no foreign language high 
schools, the Global Studies theme will be a feeder program 
for 
the International Studies program at Southeast High School and 
the Law and Public Services program at Northeast High School. 
(Tr. at 356-57, 449); KCMSD Exhibit | at § 11 p. 37. Parents 
and teachers believe the Global Studies theme is ideal for 
Northeast, (Tr. at 438-39), and with thirteen different nationali- 
ties represented at the school the program is appropriate (Tr. at 
453-52). The Court will approve the Global Studies theme at 
Northeast Middle. The Court will also approve the following 
Global Studies them budgets proposed by the KCMSD: $477,184 
in 1989-90, $805,690 for 1990-91, and $792,350 for 1991-92. 
The State has three bases of opposition to the KCMSD’s 
proposal to defer the Southeast Elementary II Gifted and Tal- 
ented theme to a school in a later phase and to replace the theme 
with a second Montessori school 
at Southeast Il. First, 
the State 
argues that the KCMSD is facing a shortage of appropriately 
certified Montessori staff; second, 
the State believes it is unnec- 
essary to assign the Gifted and Talented theme to a school to be 
opened in a later phase; and third, the State argues that the 
Montessori school will reduce the capacity of Southeast III, but 
the KCMSD has not indicated how this will affect overall 
capacity and building needs. The KCMSD 
is currently studying 
the gifted and talented program and would like to consider the 
gifted and talented magnet at the same time. (Tr. at 342). The 
Montessori theme has been very attractive and successful in 
desegregating at New Faxon. (Tr. at 342, 425, 460-61). Mr. 
Frank Vincent, the principal at New Faxon, testified that he 
agreed with the KCMSD’s request for another Montessori pro- 
gram and that it would help the KCMSD meet its desegregation 
needs and the needs of the children. (Tr. at 461). The LRMP 
States that “data on initial enrollment at the Montessori. . 
elementary [magnet] may indicate that [it] should be replicated 
or modified.” LRMP 
at 6. The Court will approve the deferral 

— A-298 — 
of the Southeast III Gifted and Talented theme to a school in a 
later phase and the replacement of that theme with a Montessori 
theme at Southeast III. The Court will approve the following 
budgets proposed by the KCMSD for the Montessori theme: 
$182,293 for 1989-90, $487,027 for 1990-91 and $554,429 for 
1991-92. 
PLAINTIFFS’ MOTION TO MODIFY 
PRIOR REMEDIAL ORDERS 
Martin Luther King, Jr., Middle School 
Plaintiffs request a one-year delay in the scheduled September 
1989 opening of the Latin Grammer middle magnet at Martin 
Luther King, Jr., Middle School to enable the KCMSD to 
complete educational planning and to execute an effective re- 
cruiting campaign. The KCMSD responded with a proposal! to 
open King in 1989-90 at an annex with only a sixth grade for 125 
students to provide continuity for fifth grade students at Pinkerton 
Latin Grammer Elementary. Plaintiffs agreed to the KCMSD’s 
proposal. The DMC declined to support the opening of the King 
Middle sixth grade at its May 18, 1989, meeting. DMC Letter of 
May 18, 1989, to the Court at 3. 
The Court cannot approve the proposal to open the sixth grade 
at King Middle School. As of May 31, 1989, only 15 students 
were expected to enroll at the King sixth grade in 1989-90 while 
the school’s capacity is 125 students. Magnet School Placement 
Status Report 
of May 31, 1989. The KCMSD filed a supplemen- 
tal memorandum on June 26, 1989, indicating that 35 students 
were expected to enroll at King in 1989-90. KCMSD’s Supple- 
mental Memorandum Regarding Martin Luther King, Jr. Latin 
Grammer 
Sixth Grade Center at 3. To attempt 
to open such an 
underutilized facility would result in a waste of time, effort and 
expense 
by all involved. The problems and delays in the opening 
of King are the result of poor planning by the KCMSD and a lack 
of continuity in the KCMSD personnel involved with King. Dr. 
Se 
nn 
ee 
— A-299 — 
Joan Caulfield, assistant superintendent who worked on the 
King sixth grade proposal, testified that she had only become 
aware of the difficulty at King in attracting a desegregated 
enrollment two or three weeks prior to the hearing and that she 
would 
be leaving the KCMSD 
on June 30, 1989. (Tr. at 190, 193, 
201). The Court has expressed concerns regarding the KCMSD’s 
planning process at the hearing and previously in this order. See 
supra p. 16. The Court stated at the hearing: 
And what has occurred is we have penalized 
some 625 
students through lack of proper planning, is that about it? 
Had we opened that sixth, seventh and eighth grade middle 
school we would have had 850 or 900 students? ... Well, 
somewhere along the line, someone in the administration of 
the Kansas City School District should have recognized 
that there was not proper planning being done. . 
. 
. What I 
am trying to do, I am trying to point out this sort of thing has 
got to be eliminated. . 
. 
. There is someone in the Kansas 
City School system that should know where that responsi- 
bility lies. 
we. at 205-07). Such poor planning cannot be tolerated any 
onger. 
Dr. Caulfield testified that it would be “extremely difficult” to 
fully open King in 1989-90. (Tr. at 196). Therefore, the Court 
has no alternative but to reluctantly grant the plaintiffs’ original 
proposal for a one-year delay in the opening of King. The 
KCMSD is directed to submit to the Court proposed budgets for 
King for 1989-90, 1990-91 and 1991-92 
on or before August 31, 
1989, Once again, the Court warns that those person responsible 
for the King Middle School planning process may be held in 
contempt of Court if the planning process for King is not 
improved and grades six through eight are not implemented as 
planned 
at King in 1990-91. If proper planning does not occur, 
desegregation funds will not be approved for King. 

—~ 
Salary Study 
Plaintiffs propose a comprehensive study of the salary rates 
and structure by which the KCMSD compensates certified and 
non-certified employees arguing that the salary structure may be 
impeding the KCMSD’s ability to implement the desegregation 
plan. The State counters that the KCMSD’s personnel problems 
may be unrelated to salaries. Dr. Stewert testified that the study 
needs to be more thorough and include job satisfaction, profes- 
sional working conditions, environment and fringe benefits. (Tr. 
at 804). The study should also analyze the tuition reimbursement 
program, staff development, reduced class time, etc. (Tr. at 805). 
Subsequently, plaintiffs proposed to amend their motion to 
incorporate Dr. Stewert’s [sic] suggestions. Plaintiffs request 
that the study be conducted by an independent organization with 
the contract awarded and administered by the DMC. Plaintiffs 
request that the DMC be allowed to submit a proposed budget to 
the Court. 
It is apparent that the KCMSD has had difficulty in both hiring 
and retaining qualified personnel. See, e.g., Plaintiffs’ Exhibit 1 
at 2-3; (Tr. at 190, 504, 
516). Although Dr. Stewert testified that 
such problems are not unique to the KCMSD, (Tr. at 803), such 
problems do affect the desegregation plan. 
There are . . 
. instances where the KCMSD has experienced 
difficulty in hiring teachers and administrators needed to 
implement the desegregation orders, due, in significant 
part, to salary considerations. 
* 
* * 
It is difficult to determine to what extent the KCMSD may 
have been impaired by salary considerations in its ability to 
implement the desegregation orders. However, it is reason- 
able to conclude that there has been some impairment 
resulting from salary factors. 
Much of the anecdotal 
— A-301 — 
information 
. 
. 
. describes difficulties in hiring and/or 
retaining administrators in magnet schools during the mag- 
net planning period or in the critical first two years of 
implementation. 
Plaintiffs’ Exhibit 1 at 3. The KCMSD School Board is con- 
cerned about is ability to implement the desegregation plan in 
light of the salary structure. (Tr. at 503-04). Dr. Stewart testified 
that the KCMSD frequently should conduct an analysis of its 
ability to attract and retain professional staff. (Tr. at 803). 
Because such an analysis will affect the implementation of the 
desegregation plan, the Court will approve plaintiffs’ request for 
a study 
of the KCMSD’s ability to hire and retain staff. The study 
is to be conducted by an independent organization and to be 
administered by the DMC. The DMC is directed to submit a 
proposed budget to the Court for approval as soon as the 
necessary information can be obtained and compiled. 
New Positions 
Plaintiffs propose the creation of two positions for a green- 
house technician and an animal technician for 1989-90 and two 
positions for a science librarian and a planetarium manager for 
Southwest High School in 1990-91. 
Plaintiffs argue that the 
greenhouse technician is needed to supervise and maintain the 
eight greenhouses mandated by the LRMP. The State opposes 
the position by arguing that existing curriculum coordinators, 
resource teachers and staff can operate the greenhouses. Each 
greenhouse is very different and each contains a variety of things 
that can go wrong. (Tr. at 540, 634, 647). No one 
is responsible 
for taking care of the greenhouses during the summer and on 
weekends and holidays. (Tr. at 541, 634-35). A greenhouse 
technician could alleviate these problems and allow the green- 
houses to be utilized to their fullest extent by teachers and 
students. The technician can also help the KCMSD avoid costly 
greenhouse repairs, possibly resulting in an overall total savings 
to the KCMSD. The Court will approve the creation of the 

— A-302 — 
position of greenhouse technician and will also approve plain- 
tiffs’ proposed budget of $31,250 for the position. 
Plaintiffs argue that an animal technician is needed to help 
supervise and maintain the LRMP animal facilities, to develop 
the LRMP Zoo Education Project and to work with the “critter 
program.” The State argues that animal care should be provided 
by previously funded KCMSD personnel or volunteers. The 
KCMSD must comply with city codes and government regula- 
tions regarding animal care. (Tr. at 548-50). A technician could 
assure that the KCMSD complies with all codes and regulations 
and could also diagnose and care for sick animals, (Tr. at 551, 
601-02), along with providing general care for the animals. The 
technician would be responsible for the care of the animals 
during the summer and on weekends and holidays. Additionally, 
a technician can provide economy and efficiency in the procure- 
ment of animals, food, bedding and housing. (Tr. at621-22). The 
Court will approve the creation of the position of animal techni- 
cian and will also approve plaintiffs’ proposed budget of $31,250 
for the position. 
Plaintiffs argue that a science librarian is necessary for 1990- 
91 at Southwest High School because the science library man- 
dated by the LRMP is separate from the main library and, 
therefore, one librarian cannot oversee both libraries simulta- 
neously. The State counters that the LRMP did not intend for a 
separate science library to be constructed and that other school 
personnel can service the library with the help of one librarian. 
The science library is located near the science labs on a different 
floor than the main library and in the opposite corner of the 
building. (Tr. at 553, 649). Two librarians would be required to 
keep both libraries open at the same time. (Tr. at 554A, 649, 654, 
671). The science librarian can also aid students in science and 
math research. (Tr. at 554). The Court will approve the position 
of science librarian at Southwest and will approve the proposed 
1990-91 budget of $39,000. Nonetheless, it does appear to the 
— A-303 — 
Court that the two separate libraries at Southwest are somewhat 
inefficient. The Court cautions that in the future more efficient 
planning is necessary for such capital improvements; otherwise, 
desegregation funds for such inefficiencies will not be approved. 
Plaintiffs request funding for the position of planetarium 
manager at Southwest High School in 1990-91 arguing that the 
position is necessary for operating the expensive planetarium 
equipment, developing demonstrations and presentations and 
caring for and maintaining the equipment. The State counters 
that one development funds could be used to train existing 
personne! to operate the planetarium. A planetarium manager 
could design planetarium programs ial teens the technical 
aspects of the equipment. (Tr. at 559, 561, 661-62, 673). The 
manager could also take care of and maintain the equipment. (Tr. 
at 662). The position would allow full utilization of the plan- 
etarium facilities. (Tr. at 561-62, 673). Ms. Marilyn Miles, a 
curriculum coordinator for the KCMSD who operated a plan- 
etarium for Six years, testified that the planetarium could not be 
operated without a planetarium manager.(Tr at 661). The Court 
will approve the position of planetarium manager at Southwest 
and the proposed 1990-91 budget of $45,000.

Southeast Elementary I 
At its meeting of May 24, 1989, the DMC moved to advise the 
Court to order the postponement of the implementation of the 
Southeast Elementary I Classical Greek program to the fall of 
1990. DMC Letter 
of May 24, 1989, tothe Court at 2. As of May 
31, 1989, Southeast had an expected enrollment of 23 students 
with a capacity for 438 students. Magnet School Placement 
Status Report of May 31, 1989. Based upon these figures and the 
DMC’s 10-0 vote in favor of postponing the opening of South- 
east I, the Court does not believe that Southeast I can be 
successfully opened in 1989-90. Therefore, the Court will order 
that the opening of Southeast Elementary I be postponed until 
1990-91. Based upon this ruling, the KCMSD is directed to 
submit proposed budgets for Southeast I for 1989-90, 1990-91 
—" to the Court for approval on or before August 31, 
STATE’S MOTION TO STRIKE 
The State filed a motion to strike plaintiffs’ reply brief arguing 
that plaintiffs are attempting to bring in new evidence by affida- 
vits after the hearing and that the State has not had an opportunity 
to examine plaintiffs’ affiants on the matters discussed in their 
affidavits. Plaintiffs respond that the reply brief and affidavits 
are appropriate pursuant to Fed.R.Civ.P. 43(e). Fed.R.Civ.P. 
43(e) provides: “When a motion is based on facts not appearing 
of record the court may hear the matter on affidavits presented by 
the respective parties, but the court 
may direct that the matter be 
heard wholly or partly on oral testimony or depositions.” This 

—=— 
= 
rule gives the Court discretion to choose between hearing oral 
testimony or taking affidavits in making factual judgments on 
the motion in question. Murray v. Kunzig, 462 F.2d 871, 878 
(D.C. Cir. 1972), rev'd on other grounds, 415 U.S. 61 (1974). 
Thus, the Court may decline to accept affidavits that are offered 
and insist on oral testimony. Jd. The Court will not decline to 
accept affidavits and insist on oral testimony in this instance. 
Therefore, the State’s motion to strike plaintiffs’ reply brief will 
be denied.

APPENDIX K 
IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE WESTERN DISTRICT OF MISSOURI 
WESTERN DIVISION 
Case No. 77-0420-CV-W-4 
KALIMA JENKINS, et ai, 
Plaintiffs, 
v. 
STATE OF MISSOURL, et ai., 
Defendants. 
ORDER 
(Filed: April 23, 1990] 
Upon consideration of the Joint Stipulation of All Parties 
Concerning Desegregation Budget for Year VI (“Joint Stipula- 
tion”), filed by the plaintiffs, KCMSD, the State of Missouri 
defendants, and AFT Local Union 691, and in consideration of 
the entire record herein, it is hereby 
ORDERED, that the following components, as presented in 
KCMSD’s Motion for Approval of Desegregation Plan for 
1990-91 and the Joint Stipulation, are approved, with budgets

APPENDIX L 
UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE WESTERN DISTRICT OF MISSOURI 
WESTERN DIVISION 
Case No. 77-0420-CV-W-4 
KALIMA JENKINS, et al, 
Plaintiffs, 
v. 
STATE OF MISSOURI, et al. 
Defendants. 
ORDER 
(Filed: May 14, 1990] 
A voluntary interdistrict transfer (VIT) proposal has been 
presented to the court by the plaintiffs and the School District of 
Kansas City, Missouri (KCMSD). Upon the joint suggestion of 
all parties 
to this cause of action, the State of Missouri defendants 
are ordered to (1) meet with representatives of public school 
districts having less than twenty-five percent (25%) minorities 
enrolled and located within Clay, Platte, and Jackson Counties 
and with the parties to this case, (2) provide to them and explain 
the proposed VIT plan attached hereto, (3) obtain agreements of 
the school districts to participate in the VIT plan, and the number 
of students each will accept in September, 1990, 
or their com- 
ments and/or suggestions for change, and (4) report on the 
State’s discharge of these duties within thirty (30) days of this 
order. Suggestions or arguments by any entity as to the contents 
of the VIT plan will not be used as evidence of racial animus in 
any future proceeding. This court will hold a hearing on the 
status of this proposed VIT plan on the __ day of June, 1990 
beginning at 

— A-318 — 
/s/ Russell G. Clark 
Russell G. Clark, Judge 
United States District Court 
Date: May 14, 1990 
Kansas City Metropolitan Area 
Voluntary Interdistrict Transfer Plan 
1. 
The Role of the State 
A. The State of Missouri shall establish and fund a voluntary 
‘nterdistrict transfer (“VIT”) program in the Kansas City metro- 
politan area. The State shall pay for the transportation and tuition 
costs of any Kansas City, Missouri Schoo! District ( KCMSD’ ) 
minority student who wishes to transfer from 4 school within 
KCMSD in which their race is in the majority (with preferences 
for students from schools with enrollments of 90% or more 
minority students) to a school in another school district in Clay, 
Platte or Jackson County in which minorities constitute less than 
25% of the student body. The tuition payment shall be reason- 
ably determined by each participating school district. The a 
shall also pay KCMSD one-half of the foundation allotment or 
each student who transfers from the KCMSD under this plan or, 
in the alternative, if KCMSD sends more students than it re- 
ceives, State aid based on the District's enrollment for the second 
prior year. The method of selection will be chosen annually by 
the KCMSD. The State shall be required 
to pay aspen 
for up to 1,200 students in the 1990-91 school year, ~ 
students in the 1991-92 school year, 3,600 students in the 1 : 
93 school year, 4,800 students in the 1993-94 school year an 
6,000 students in the 1994-95 school year and thereafter. 
B. The State shall be required to fund the salary and expenses 
of one full-time counselor for every 50 KCMSD students who 
transfer to surrounding school districts (“SSDs”) in the three- 
— A-319 — 
county area under Section I.A. In addition, the State shall fund 
the staff development programs described in Section IIIL.E. 
C. 
The State shall fund the Interdistrict Recruiting and 
Counseling Committee (“IRCC”) described in Section III. 
D. In future years, the State’s funding responsibilities shall 
continue and the State shall continue to encourage full participa- 
tion by all SSDs. In particular, the State shall encourage SSDs 
to increase the number of transfers they accept in future years so 
that collectively they will meet the goals of Section I.A. 
Il. 
Surrounding School Districts (“SSD”) 
Each of the school districts in the three-county area described 
in Section I.A shall be eligible to participate in this plan if it is 
willing to accept an adequate number of transfer students to 
avoid isolation and to follow the provisions in Sections III and 
IV. 
Ill. Interdistrict Recruiting and Counseling, Committee 
(“IRCC”) 
A. The IRCC shall be composed of one representative of each 
participating SSD, one KCMSD representative , one representa- 
tive of AFT Local 691, one representative of the State and a 
representative of the plaintiffs. Each member of the IRCC shall 
have one vote and decisions will be made by majority vote. The 
committee shall select its own chair. 
B. The IRCC shall meed within thirty days of the promulga- 
tion of this plan and shall present a proposed budget, including 
necessary staff, to the DMC within forty-five days of promulga- 
tion of this plan. The DMC shall present its recommendations to 
the Court within fifteen days of the IRCC’s report. 
C. The IRCC shall coordinate among participating SSDs the 
dissemination 
of information about available transfer opportuni- 
ties and the establishment of application and acceptance dates 

— A-320 — 
that will allow transfer students to make reasonable decisions 
about where to attend school in a timely manner. 
D. The IRCC shall hire and supervise the counselors provided 
under Section I.B, who shall assist in assuring a smooth transi- 
tion for transfer students and spend a proportionate amount of 
time at the suburban schools in which KCMSD transfer students 
are enrolled. 
E. The IRCC will be responsible for assuring that an effective 
Multicultural/Human Relations Staff Development program is 
provided for the staffs of all SSDs which elect to participate in 
this program. This will include, among other things, a program 
offered in each SSD before the opening of school in the fall to 
sensitize school district staff to the needs of transfer students. 
Such programs shall continue annually so long as the IRCC 
determines they are necessary. 
F. The IRCC shall facilitate participating districts’ efforts to 
recruit minority transfers to the SSDs and non-minority appli- 
cants to KCMSD magnet schools and shall ensure that sufficient 
recruiting activities are conducted either by the participating 
districts or the IRCC itself. 
G.The IRCC shall report four times each year to the DMC by 
February 1, May 1, August 1 and November 1 about progress and 
problems in implementing the plan. The IRCC shall include in 
all of its reports to the DMC and the Court the number of students 
participating in the current year and expected to participate in the 
subsequent year, enrollments by grade, race, school and resi- 
dent/transfer status, information about its counseling and re- 
cruiting efforts, comments on transportation under the plan and 
any information specifically requested by the DMC or the Court 
or any relevant IRCC concerns about the plan. 
H. The IRCC shall also develop standards to propose to the 
DMC and the Court to govern the transportation system de- 
scribed in Section V. 
— A-321 —

VII. Research and Evaluation 
The DMC shall be responsible for research and evaluation 
concerning the effectiveness of the Kansas City Metropolitan 
Area Voluntary Interdistrict Transfer Plan and shall report its 
conclusions to the Court.

APPENDIX M 
IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE WESTERN DISTRICT Gy MISSOURI 
WESTERN DIVISION 
No. 77-0420-CV-W-4 
KALIMA JENKINS, et al, 
Plaintiffs, 
Vv. 
STATE OF MISSOURL, et al., 
Defendants. 
ORDER 
(Filed: June 26, 1990] 
Before the Court is plaintiffs’ motion for Year VI 
budget modifications and KCMSD’s motion for co an 
desegregation plan for 1990-91. Subsequently the parties filed 
a joint stipulation regarding certain issues in the KCMSD’s and 
plaintiffs’ motions. On April 23, 1990, the Court entered an 
order in accordance with the stipulations. Subsequently the State 
filed amemorandum 
on the remaining Year 
VI budget issues and 
the KCMSD and plaintiffs filed a joint response to the State’s 
memorandum. The State filed a reply memorandum 
and the 
KCMSD and plaintiffs filed a joint response. Plaintiffs’ motion 
for Year VI program and budget modifications and the KCMSD’s 
motion for approval of desegregation plan for 1990-91 will be 
granted as set forth below. 
AAA 
In its order of July 25, 1988, this Court 
ved a 1990- 
AAA budget of $6,574,673, which the KCMSD solely ~< 
sible for $1,963,535. Jenkins v. State of 
Missouri, Order of July 
25, 1988, at 21-24 (W.D. Mo. 1988). The KCMSD seeks a 
slightly reduced 1990-91 AAA budget of $6,569,389. In addi- 

— A-324 — 
tion, the KCMSD proposes to use one of the Court-approved 
counselors to provide counseling services at Delano and the 
Teenage-Parent Center, two of the KCMSD’s special schools. 
The State argues that the time has come for the KCMSD to solely 
bear 
the costs of maintaining 
AAA status. The KCMSD counters 
that the achievement and retention of AAA status is a proper 
desegregation expense. In addition, the KCMSD states that its 
operating budget is not sufficient to meet the requirements of 
AAA status. The retention of AAA status is a proper desegrega- 
tion expense. Liddell v. Board of Education of City of St. Louis, 
758 F.2d 290, 294 (8th Cir. 1985); Liddell v. State of Missouri, 
731 F.2d 1294, 1318 (8th Cir.), cert. denied, 469 U.S. 816 
(1984); Jenkins, Order of July 25, 1988, at 24; id., Order of July 
6, 1987, at 10. Therefore, the Court will approve a AAA budget 
of $6,569,389 in 1990-91 with the KCMSD solely responsible 
for $3,111,035. In addition, 
the Court will approve 
the use of one 
counselor to provide counseling services at two of the KCMSD’s 
special schools. 
Reduction in Class Size 
The Court previously approved a reduction in class size 
budget of $8,787,191 for 1990-91 stating that “[iJf student 
enroliments justify additional funding for . . 
. 1990-91, KCMSD 
may move the Court for such funding.” Id., Order of July 25, 
1988, at 8. The KCMSD has determined that it can meet the 
Court-ordered class sizes with fewer teachers than the Court 
approved in the July 25, 1988, order, resulting in a 1990-91 
budget request of $4,683,986. The KCMSD seeks to eliminate 
31 approved positions at the elementary level, 38.5 positions at 
the middle school level and 72.5 positions at the high school 
level. The State does not oppose the KCMSD’s budget request. 
Therefore, the Court will approve a reduction in class size budget 
of $4,683,986 for 1990-91 with the KCMSD solely responsible 
for $3,512,990 pursuant to the Court’s order of July 5, 1989. Id., 
Order of July 5, 1989, at 6-8. 
— A-325 — 
Full-Day Kindergarten 
The Court previously approved a full-day kindergarten bud- 
get of $1,773,185 for 1990-91 stating that “[i]f student enroll- 
ments justify increased or decreased funding for . .. 1990-91, 
the 
Court will make an appropriate modification.” /d., Order of July 
25, 1988, at 14. The KCMSD has not requested a modification 
of the previously approved budget for 1990-91. The State does 
not oppose the full-day kindergarten budget request. Therefore, 
the Court will approve a full-day kindergarten budget of 
$1,773,185 for 1990-91 with the KCMSD responsible for 
$443,296 of that budget. 
1986-87 Magnet Schools 
Previously the Court refused to approve the KCMSD’s 1990- 
91 budget for the 1986-87 magnets because the budget did not 
properly delineate between base operating costs and incremental 
magnet costs. /d., Order 
of July 5, 1989, at 3-4; id., Order 
of July 
25, 1988, at 20-21. The KCMSD now seeks approval of a 1990- 
91 1986-87 magnet budget of $15,694,833 with $10,385,602 of 
this budget deemed a desegregation expense allocated 100% to 
the KCMSD. 
In 1988-89 and 1989-90 the Court approved total 1986-87 
magnet budgets as a desegregation expense 
and subsequently 
allowed the State a credit in the amount of base costs necessary 
to operate a traditional program at each school against the 
amount of their total desegregation obligation 
for 1988-89 and 
1990-91, respectively, 
because base costs for the 1986-87 mag- 
nets are not a proper desegregation expense and the KCMSD 
should be solely liable for such costs out of its operating budget. 
Id., Order of October 18, 1989, at 2-3; id., Order of November 2, 
1989, at 4-5; id., Order 
of January 23, 1990, at 1-6. The State 
argues that the Court should approve only the 1986-87 magnet 
school incremental costs as a desegregation expense in 1990-91 
with the base costs of these schools included in the KCMSD’s 

— A-326 — 
1990-91 operating budget. However, on February 1, 1990, this 
Court entered an order which suspended and stayed the Court’s 
orders of October 18, 1989, November 2, 1989, and January 23, 
1990. Therefore, the Court finds that the proposed budget of 
$15,694,833 for 1990-91 is reasonable and will approve such 
budget as a desegregation expense with $10,385,602 allocated 
100% to the KCMSD. If the February 1, 1990, stay is subse- 
quently lifted, the State shall be allowed a credit in the amount 
of the base costs necessary to operate a traditional program at 
each school against the amount of their total desegregation 
obligation for 1990-91. 
Base Budgets 
The KCMSD requests a 1990-91 budget for base budgets for 
New Paseo Middle School, Lincoln Middle School, New West 
Elementary, 
New Gladstone Elementary, Middle School I, Middle 
School II and Elementary III. In 1990-91 the KCMSD will also 
be opening four additional elementary magnet schools but is not 
requesting base budget funding for these schools because it is 
closing four traditional elementary schools in 1990-91. The 
State argues that the time has come to no longer fund the base 
budget magnets as a separate desegregation expense, but instead 
treat them like the schools under the LRMP. The Court has 
previously approved base budget components as a desegregation 
expense. Id., Order of July 5, 1989, at 4; id., Order of July 25, 
1988, at 18-19. The base budget component is a proper deseg- 
regation expense because the district would not have been 
required to open and operate these new magnets but for the 
Court-ordered LRMP. The State offers no basis to the Court to 
revise the prior rulings regarding the base budget component. 
The State appears to be attempting to relitigate the issue of costs 
avoided elsewhere, which was addressed by this Court in its 
order of May 24, 1989. Therefore, the Court will approve the 
base budget component of $15,358,428 as a desegregation 
expense with the KCMSD’s obligation totaling $3,839,607. 
— A-327 — 
However, the State will be allowed a credit for the amount of 
costs avoided elsewhere as addressed by the Court in its order of 
May “. es The tore directs the KCMSD to submit the 
amount of costs avoided 
elsewhere for 1990-91 
are available. 
aiken 
Security 
The maintenance plan approved by the Court 
as 
of i 
September 15, 1987, order provides $170,022 for 1990-91 he 
1991-92 toupgrade the KCMSD security program. The KCMSD 
NOW proposes to increase the security budget to 
i 
1990-91 and $428,339 
in 1991-92 amelie a eee a 
of the KCMSD security department as thefts become more 
costly as a result of the influx of educational equipment from the 
desegregation programs. The KCMSD argues that the proposal 
will provide a critical link in the recruiting effort and enable the 
KCMSD to protect more effectively the equipment that has been 
purchased in furtherance of the desegregation plan. 
The 
KCMSD' s original proposal requested desegregation funds for 
ten additional site-based security guards, one new manager of 
site-based security officers, one new investigator and five addi- 
tional patrol officer dispatchers. The KCMSD also proposed to 
extend the contracts of all sixteen site-based security guards 
from ten months to twelve months. The DMC took no exception 
to the ten additional site-based security guards and one new 
investigator, but did take exception to one new manager of site- 
based security officers nd five additional patrol officer dispatch- 
ers. DMC Minutes of March 19, 1990, at 10. The DMC 
determined that the non-site-based security initiatives proposed 
by the KCMSD were “too staff intensive,” and urged the KCMSD 
‘to develop a more cost-effective and efficient proposal which 
addresses internal security as well as external.” 
Jd. In response 
the KCMSD eliminated the site-based manager position from 
the proposal, but retained the dispatchers “because of their 
importance to the District’s effort to combat property losses.” 

— A-328 — 
KCMSD’s Motion for Approval of Desegregation Plan for 
1990-91 at 27. The State responds that security is a maintenance 
function which is not a proper desegregation expense. 
The 
KCMSD counters that proper security makes the KCMSD 
schools more desegregatively attractive and that much of the 
property the KCMSD is trying to protect is needed to carry out 
the desegregation plan. 
The Court recognizes that security has become an important 
issue in the KCMSD. See Declaration of William M. Threatt, Jr., 
at { 7. Proper security is vital to the desegregative attractiveness 
of the KCMSD. See id. at { 8. During the 1988-89 school year 
alone there were 926 incidents reported to the KCMSD security 
department resulting in losses totaling $221,958. KCMSD’s 
1990-91 Year VI Desegregation Plan at 136. A large portion of 
the KCMSD’s losses are attributable to electronic equipment 
purchased to implement the desegregation plan. Declaration of 
William M. Threatt, Jr., at{ 6. The Court has previously stated: 
“The Court recognizes that the security needs of the KCMSD . 
. 
. have changed dramatically as a result of the desegregation 
plan. It would be illogical for the Court to order implementation 
of a desegregation plan calling for new and renovated facilities 
containing new equipment, supplies and furniture without ad- 
equate security to protect such investments.” Jenkins, Order of 
March 30, 1989, at 2-3. In the March 30, 1989, order the Court 
granted the KCMSD’s request to recognize summer security 
expenses as desegregation expenses. Therefore, the Court finds 
that the KCMSD’s request for security funds is a proper deseg- 
regation expense. 
However, the Court will not approve the 
KCMSD’s request for five additional patrol officer dispatchers 
to which the DMC took exception. The KCMSD shall file a 
proposed budget for security for 1990-91 and 1991-92 deleting 
the five additional patrol officer dispatchers on or before July 13, 
1990. 
— A-329 — 
Desegregation Monitoring Committee 
As this Court has stated many times before, the DMC is an arm 
of the Court and has shown itself to be an effective and impartial 
group committed to achieving the goals outlined in this Court’s 
desegregation plan. See, e.g., id., Order 
of July 5, 1989, at 26; id., 
Order of July 25, 1988, at 5. The Court cannot over emphasize 
the importance of the DMC to the Court and the desegregation 
plan. The Court will approve a DMC budget of $416,655 for 
1990-91. The Court will approve a per diem budget component 
totaling $116,300, which consists of an increase in compensable 
hours to 225 hours for the general chairperson, 188 hours 
for 
committee chairpersons and 163 hours for committee members. 
Such an increase is warranted, as the DMC members spend many 
hours on DMC matters for which they are re not compensated. 
See Attachment A. A research budget of $54,200 and an 
operating budget of $35,000 will be approved. The Court will 
approve a staff compensation budget of $211,155, which in- 
cludes a six percent salary increase for the executive secretary 
and administrative assistant, addition of benefits for the admin- 
istrative assistant and the addition of two evaluators to the staff 
to assist with the day-to-day program monitoring and general 
assessment of the implementation of the plan. The Court will 
also approve a carryover 
of the balances of the fiscal year 1990 
accounts to the 1991 fiscal year, which will result in a credit to 
the State and the KCMSD against their respective share of 
the 
funding. 
Conclusion 
The budgets approved previously in this order shall be allo- 
cated 25% to the KCMSD 
and 75% to 
the State, unless otherwise 
provided, with joint and several liability. 
Accordingly, it is hereby 

— A-330 — 
— A-331 — 
ORDERED that plaintiffs’ motion for Year VI program and 
budget modifications is granted as set forth in this order; and it 
is further 
ORDERED that the KCMSD’s motion for approval of deseg- 
regation plan for 1990-91 is granted as set forth in this order; and 
it is further 
ORDERED that the DMC’s budget request is granted as set 
forth in this order; and it is further 
ORDERED that the KCMSD shall file a proposed security 
component budget for 1990-91 and 1991-92 on or before July 13, 
1990; and it is further 
ORDERED that the KCMSD shall submit the amount of costs 
avoided elsewhere for 1990-91 as soon as they are available. 
/s/ Russell G. Clark
